arXiv:math/0011114v6  [math.AG]  24 Jul 2001
Symplectic reﬂection algebras, Calogero-Moser space,
and deformed Harish-Chandra homomorphism
Pavel Etingof and Victor Ginzburg
Abstract
To any ﬁnite group Γ ⊂Sp(V ) of automorphisms of a symplectic vector space V we
associate a new multi-parameter deformation, Hκ, of the algebra C[V ]#Γ, smash product
of Γ with the polynomial algebra on V .
The parameter κ runs over points of Pr, where
r = number of conjugacy classes of symplectic reﬂections in Γ.
The algebra Hκ, called a
symplectic reﬂection algebra, is related to the coordinate ring of a Poisson deformation of
the quotient singularity V/Γ. This leads to a symplectic analogue of McKay correspondence,
which is most complete in case of wreath-products. If Γ is the Weyl group of a root system
in a vector space h and V = h ⊕h∗, then the algebras Hκ are certain ‘rational’ degenerations
of the double aﬃne Hecke algebra introduced earlier by Cherednik.
Let Γ = Sn, the Weyl group of g = gln. We construct a 1-parameter deformation of the
Harish-Chandra homomorphism from D(g)g, the algebra of invariant polynomial diﬀerential
operators on gln, to the algebra of Sn-invariant diﬀerential operators with
rational coeﬃ-
cients on the space Cn of diagonal matrices. The second order Laplacian on g goes, under the
deformed homomorphism, to the Calogero-Moser diﬀerential operator on Cn, with rational
potential. Our crucial idea is to reinterpret the deformed Harish-Chandra homomorphism as
a homomorphism: D(g)g ։ spherical subalgebra in Hκ, where Hκ is the symplectic reﬂection
algebra associated to the group Γ = Sn. This way, the deformed Harish-Chandra homomor-
phism becomes nothing but a description of the spherical subalgebra in terms of ‘quantum’
Hamiltonian reduction.
In the ‘classical’ limit κ →∞, our construction gives an isomorphism between the spheri-
cal subalgebra in H∞and the coordinate ring of the Calogero-Moser space. We prove that all
simple H∞-modules have dimension n!, and are parametrised by points of the Calogero-Moser
space. The family of these modules forms a distinguished vector bundle on the Calogero-
Moser space, whose ﬁbers carry the regular representation of Sn. Moreover, we prove that
the algebra H∞is isomorphic to the endomorphism algebra of that vector bundle.

1
Introduction
Given an action of a ﬁnite group Γ on a smooth aﬃne algebraic variety X, one can study
the algebraic structure of the orbifold X/Γ through its coordinate ring C[X]Γ, the ring of
Γ-invariant regular functions on X. It is known, however, that if the Γ-action on X is not
free the (possibly singular) space X/Γ does not necessarily reﬂect the features of the Γ-
action quite adequately. For instance, one is led to introduce various ”stringy” topological
invariants, cf.
[Ba], which do not coincide with the ordinary topological invariants of
X/Γ, but are rather related to certain ”resolutions” of X/Γ. A standard approach to
this problem, see e.g. [Co], is to replace the commutative coordinate ring C[X]Γ by the
smash-product algebra C[X]#Γ. The two algebras are Morita equivalent, hence lead to
equivalent theories, provided the Γ-action on X is free, but are not equivalent in general.
Although the algebra C[X]#Γ is non-commutative, it is believed that the ”right” geometry
of the Γ-action on X can be read oﬀfrom the ”non-commutative algebraic geometry” of
C[X]#Γ. This is the approach that we adopt in the present paper.
Speciﬁcally, we will study deformations of some interesting orbifolds X/Γ through
non-commutative deformations of the corresponding algebra C[X]#Γ. We will see that
a systematic use of this ideology leads, via Morita equivalence, to various new results in
conventional ”commutative” algebraic geometry which have been unaccessible by purely
”commutative” methods.
Another fascinating feature of our approach is that genuine deformations of X/Γ
coming from ”commutative” algebraic varieties stand on an equal footing with purely
”quantum” deformations that are not associated with any algebraic varieties whatsoever.
”Commutative” and ”quantum” deformations only diﬀer by the values of deformation
parameters. Thus, ”quantum” deformations may be viewed as quantizations of ”commu-
tative” ones, and Kontsevich’s quantization plays a role here. On the other hand, the
algebras arising in such ”quantum” deformations are closely related to the algebras of in-
variant polynomial diﬀerential operators on semisimple Lie algebras and Quiver varieties.
Thus, on the ”quantum” side, we enter the realm of representation theory, which was our
original motivation.
Symplectic reﬂection algebras.
Let V be a ﬁnite dimensional vector space over C,
and TV its tensor algebra. Let Γ ⊂GL(V ) be a ﬁnite group, and (TV )#Γ the smash-
product of TV with CΓ, the group algebra of Γ.
Given a skew-symmetric C-bilinear
pairing κ : V × V →CΓ, put
Hκ := (TV #Γ)/I⟨x ⊗y −y ⊗x −κ(x, y) ∈T 2V ⊕CΓ⟩x,y∈V ,
(1.1)
where I⟨. . . ⟩stands for the two-sided ideal in (TV )#Γ generated by the indicated set.
Thus, Hκ is an associative algebra.
In the special case κ = 0, the situation simpliﬁes drastically since all the generators
x ∈V commute with each other. The resulting algebra is therefore isomorphic to the
smash-product algebra: Hκ=0 = (SV ) # Γ. Note that this algebra has a natural grading
obtained by placing CΓ in grade degree zero, and V in grade degree 1.

In general, if κ ̸= 0, the deﬁning relations, see (1.1), become inhomogeneous, hence
assigning CΓ grade degree zero, and V grade degree 1, one only gets an increasing ﬁl-
tration F•(Hκ) on the corresponding algebra Hκ, such that, for any i, j ≥0, we have:
Fi(Hκ) · Fj(Hκ) ⊂Fi+j(Hκ). Let gr(Hκ) = L
i Fi(Hκ)/Fi−1(Hκ) denote the associated
graded algebra. It is immediate from (1.1) that, for any x, y ∈V ⊂gr(Hκ), in gr(Hκ) we
have: xy −yx = 0. Therefore the tautological imbedding: V
֒→gr(Hκ) extends to a
well-deﬁned and surjective graded algebra homomorphism: Hκ=0 = (SV )#Γ −→gr(Hκ).
We say that Poincar´e-Birkhoﬀ-Witt (PBW-)property holds for Hκ if this morphism is an
isomorphism.
From now on we assume, in addition, that V is a symplectic vector space with sym-
plectic 2-form ω, and that Γ ⊂Sp(V ). The triple (V, ω, Γ) is said to be indecomposable
if there is no ω-orthogonal direct sum decomposition: V = V1 ⊕V2, where Vi are Γ-stable
proper symplectic vector subspaces in V .
Deﬁnition 1.2 An element s ∈Γ is called a symplectic reﬂection if
rk(Id −s) = 2.
Let S denote the set of symplectic reﬂections in Γ. The group Γ acts on S by conju-
gation. For each s ∈S, write ωs for the (possibly degenerate) skew-symmetric form on V
which coincides with ω on Im(Id−s), and has Ker(Id −s) as the radical (recall that there
is an ω-orthogonal direct sum decomposition V = Im(Id −s) ⊕Ker(Id −s)).
Theorem 1.3 Assume that (V, ω, Γ) is an indecomposable triple. Then, PBW-property
holds for Hκ if and only if there exists a constant t ∈C, and an Ad Γ-invariant function
c : S →C , s 7→cs, such that the pairing κ has the form:
κ(x, y) = t · ω(x, y) · 1 +

the triple (V, ω, Γ) is decomposable.
(ii) An analogue of Theorem 1.3 holds in a more general setup, where V is any (not
necessarily symplectic) vector space, and Γ ⊂GL(V ) is a ﬁnite subgroup containing
no elements s ∈Γ such that rk(Id −s) = 1.

Example:
Kleinian singularity.
Let Γ ⊂SL2(C) be a ﬁnite subgroup acting on
V = C2.
The action on V ∖{0} is free, and any element of Γ ∖{1} is a symplectic
reﬂection. This case has been studied by Crawley-Boevey and Holland [CBH].

Quasi-classical case: κ = (0, c).
Assume next that t = 0 and c ̸= 0. The algebra
H0,c , c ̸= 0, has some very interesting special features making it quite diﬀerent from
other members of the family {Hκ}κ∈C⊕C . First of all, the algebra H0,c has a large center,
Z0,c = Z(H0,c), as opposed to the algebras Hκ for generic κ. In more detail, the standard
ﬁltration on H0,c induces a ﬁltration on Z(H0,c), and we will show that:

Theorem 1.7 Let U be an aﬃne Zariski-open subset contained in the smooth locus of
Spec Z0,c. Then we have:
(i) The sheaf RU is locally free, and the algebra HU is isomorphic to EndZU(RU ).
(ii) The algebra HU is Morita equivalent to ZU, i.e. the categories of ﬁnitely generated
HU -modules and ZU-modules are equivalent.
(iii) Any simple HU -module is isomorphic to one of the form: HU e N
ZU χ, for a certain
character χ : ZU →C. In particular, the action of the center deﬁnes a bijection between
the isomorphism classes of simple HU -modules and points of U.
(iv) Any simple HU-module has dimension |Γ| and is isomorphic, as a Γ-module, to the
regular representation of Γ.
If Spec Z0,c is itself smooth then claims (i) -(iv) hold for U = Spec Z0,c, and HU = H0,c.
Following [Alv], on the group algebra CΓ, deﬁne an increasing ﬁltration F•(CΓ) by
letting Fk(CΓ) , k ≥0, be the C-linear span of the elements g ∈Γ such that rk(Id−g) ≤k.
This ﬁltration is obviously compatible with the algebra structure on CΓ. Let F•(ZΓ) denote
the induced ﬁltration on ZΓ, the center of CΓ.

part (ii), may be viewed as a ‘symplectic generalization’ of McKay correspondence,

(i) For all t ∈C∗, except possibly a countable set, there is a natural graded algebra

Recall the result of Batyrev [Ba, Thm. 4.8] saying that, for any crepant (hence, sym-

V/Γ has a smooth symplectic resolution of singularities, d
V/Γ, then this resolution can be
deformed to a smooth aﬃne symplectic manifold. We expect that the latter coincides
with the variety Spec Z0,c, for an appropriate value of the parameter c ∈C, in particu-
lar, Spec Z0,c is smooth for generic c ∈C. Our expectation is based on Proposition 17.4
(of Appendix F) that would conjecturally imply that any Poisson deformation of V/Γ,
in particular the one constructed by Kaledin, is given by a certain variety Spec Z0,c. If
our expectations are correct, then any smooth symplectic resolution, d
V/Γ, has the same
cohomology algebra as Spec Z0,c. Thus, Theorem 1.8 would imply the conjecture in [Va].
In many concrete examples the space d
V/Γ has a natural hyper-K¨ahler structure such that
Spec Z0,c is obtained from d
V/Γ by ‘rotating’ the complex structure. Hence the two spaces
are even diﬀeomorphic, and Theorem 1.8 applies, cf. Question 17.2. This is the case, for
instance, for Γ = Sn acting diagonally on V = Cn ⊕Cn. In that case, d
V/Γ = Hilbn(C2) is
the Hilbert scheme of n points on the plane, and the corresponding result on the algebra
structure of H• Hilbn(C2)

Their approach is totally diﬀerent from ours.

Wreath-product case.
An inﬁnite series of interesting examples of groups generated
by symplectic reﬂections1 is provided by a so-called wreath-product construction. Fix a
1 see [Coh] for a classiﬁcation of ﬁnite groups generated by symplectic reﬂections.
6


===== PDF PAGE 7 =====

ﬁnite subgroup Γ ⊂SL2(C) and an integer n > 1. We write L = C2 for the tautological
2-dimensional Γ-module, form the vector space V = L⊕n with the symplectic structure ωV
induced from the standard one on L = C2, and let Sn, the Symmetric group, act on V by
permutations of the direct summands. The group Γn := Sn ⋉(Γ × Γ × . . . × Γ) ⊂Sp(V )
acts naturally on V = L⊕n and is called the wreath-product of Sn with Γ.
It is known that C(Γn), the space of class functions on the set of symplectic reﬂections
in Γn, can be identiﬁed, cf. (11.1), with ZΓ, the center of the group algebra of Γ. Thus
there is a symplectic reﬂection algebra, H0,c(Γn), attached to the triple (V, ωV , Γn), and
any parameter c ∈ZΓ ≃C(Γn).
On the other hand, let V = CΓ⊕n be the direct sum of n copies of the regular rep-
resentation of Γ. Following Nakajima [Na1,Na2], to any c ∈ZΓ one associates an aﬃne
algebraic variety of quiver data:

Rational Cherednik algebra.
All indecomposable triples (V, ω, Γ) fall into two large
groups: (1) those where V is an irreducible Γ-module, and (2) those where V = h⊕h∗is a
direct sum of two Γ-stable irreducible Lagrangian subspaces. In the second case, the action
on h by any symplectic reﬂection g ∈Γ has a pointwise ﬁxed codimension 1 hyperplane
in h, hence is a complex reﬂection in h. Thus, in case (2), saying that Γ is generated by
symplectic reﬂections amounts to saying that Γ = W is a ﬁnite complex reﬂection group
in a vector space h, acting on V = T ∗h ≃h + h∗by induced symplectic automorphisms.
Any (complex) reﬂection s ∈W induces the corresponding symplectic reﬂection of h + h∗.
In this paper, we will be mostly interested in the special case where W is the Weyl
group of a ﬁnite reduced root system R ⊂h∗. The set S of reﬂections in W forms either
one or two conjugacy classes, depending on whether all roots α ∈R are of the same
length or not. Thus, giving a W-invariant function c : S →C amounts to giving a map:
α 7→cα ∈C, where cα depends only on the length of α ∈R.
Let Ht,c be the symplectic reﬂection algebra associated to a complex number t ∈C,
a map c as above, and the vector space V = h + h∗. Explicitly, write α∨∈h for the
coroot corresponding to a root α ∈R, and sα ∈W for the reﬂection relative to α. Then,
formula (1.1) shows that the algebra Ht,c is generated by the spaces h, h∗, and the group
W, subject to the following deﬁning relations:
w · x · w−1 = w(x) , w · y · w−1 = w(y) ,
∀y ∈h , x ∈h∗, w ∈W
[x1, x2] = 0 = [y1, y2] ,
∀y1, y2 ∈h, x1 , x2 ∈h∗
[y, x] = t · ⟨y, x⟩−1
2 · P
α∈R cα · ⟨y, α⟩⟨α∨, x⟩· sα ,
∀y ∈h , x ∈h∗,
(1.15)
(the factor 1
2 on the RHS of the last equation accounts to the fact that roots α and −α
give rise to the same reﬂection).
We will refer to the algebra Ht,c above as a rational Cherednik algebra because it is
a certain ‘rational’ degeneration of the double aﬃne Hecke algebra studied by Cherednik
[Ch]. In the case of type A1, the algebra Hκ was discussed in detail in [ChM].
Olshanetsky-Perelomov [OP], have associated a Calogero-Moser type integrable system
to any Weyl group W. We show that the space Spec Z(H0,c) for the rational Cherednik
algebra attached to W is a natural completion of the phase space of that integrable sys-
tem. The aﬃne algebraic variety Spec Z(H0,c) is always an irreducible normal Gorenstein
variety with a Poisson structure. We propose to call it the Calogero-Moser space asso-
ciated to the Weyl group W, with parameter c. For Weyl groups of type: An, Bn, Cn
and suﬃciently general parameters c ∈C, the variety Spec Z(H0,c) is a smooth symplectic
manifold, by Theorem 1.13.
Relation to the double-aﬃne Hecke algebra.
The Cherednik algebra Ht,c may be
thought of as the following two-step degeneration of the double aﬃne Hecke algebra H
deﬁned in [Ch]:
H = H
elliptic
⇝H
trigonometric
⇝H
rational = Ht,c .
In more detail, let G be the Lie group corresponding to the Lie algebra g, and T a
maximal torus of G. The algebra H contains the group algebras of two (dual) lattices:
8


===== PDF PAGE 9 =====

X = Hom(C∗, T) and Y = Hom(T, C∗).
Degeneration from H to H
trigonometric amounts,
eﬀectively, to replacing the group algebra of the lattice X = Hom(C∗, T) by the Symmetric
algebra of the vector space HomC(C, Lie T) = h, while the group algebra of the second
lattice Y remains unaﬀected. Degeneration from H
trigonometric to H
rational = Ht,c is obtained
similarly, by further replacing the group algebra of the lattice Y = Hom(T, C∗) by the
Symmetric algebra of the vector space HomC(Lie T, C) = h∗.
An analogous degeneration pattern applies to other objects considered in this paper,
so that many of the constructions of the paper, for Γ being a Weyl group, have their
‘trigonometric’ and ‘elliptic’ analogues, see (1.21).
Harish-Chandra homomorphism.
The goal of Part II is to describe the spherical
subalgebra, eHt,ce, of the symplectic reﬂection algebra associated to the root system of type
A as a ‘quantum Hamiltonian reduction’ of the algebra D(gln) of polynomial diﬀerential
operators on the Lie algebra gln. A similar description conjecturally exists for the spherical
subalgebra of the symplectic reﬂection algebra associated to any wreath-product Γn, in
which case gln gets replaced by a (vector) space of representations of a Dynkin quiver
(gln is the space of n-dimensional representations of the quiver with one vertex and one
edge-loop).
We begin in a greater generality of an arbitrary complex reductive Lie algebra g. Let
h be a Cartan subalgebra of g, and W the Weyl group of (g, h). Let D(g)g be the algebra
of ad g-invariant polynomial diﬀerential operators on g, and D(h)W the algebra of W-
invariant polynomial diﬀerential operators on h. Write ∆g, resp. ∆h, for the second order
Laplacian on g, resp. on h, associated to a nondegenerate invariant bilinear form on g.
In 1964, Harish-Chandra [HC] deﬁned an algebra homomorphism Φ : D(g)g →D(h)W ,
that reduces to the restriction map: C[g]g →C[h]W , f 7→f

h, on zero order diﬀerential
operators, and such that Φ(∆g) = ∆h.
In this paper, we construct a 1-parameter deformation of the Harish-Chandra homo-
morphism Φ in the special case g = gln, and show that this deformed homomorphism may
(and should) be viewed as an isomorphism between the quantum Hamiltonian reduction
of the algebra D(gln) and the spherical subalgebra eHt,ce. To this end, in §6 we work out
the formalism of radial parts of invariant diﬀerential operators in a slightly more general
framework than treated in classical texts.
Now let h = Cn be the Cartan subalgebra of diagonal matrices in g = gln. Write
x1, . . . , xn for coordinates in Cn, and h
reg ⊂Cn for the open subset of points with pairwise
distinct coordinates. In §7 we apply the formalism of radial parts to construct a family
of algebra homomorphisms Φk : D(g)g →D(h
reg)W depending on a parameter k ∈C,
which specializes to the Harish-Chandra homomorphism Φ at k = 0. For each k ∈C,
this homomorphism Φk reduces to the restriction map: C[g]g →C[h]W , f 7→f

h, on zero
order diﬀerential operators. Further, we have: Φk(∆g) = Lk, where

A drawback of Theorem 1.17 is that elements of the algebra Bk ⊂D(h

of Bk has a very simple description that we now explain.
If R = An−1, is the root system of g = gln then all roots are of the same length, and C =
C. We treat the parameter κ = (t, c) as a point on the Riemann sphere P1 = (C ⊕C)/C∗
with homogeneous coordinates (c : t), i.e., as a complex number κ = c/t ∈C ∪{∞}
(as will be explained after (2.4), rescaling: (t, c) 7→(r · t, r · c) , r ∈C∗, has no essential
eﬀect on the algebra Ht,c). Thus, in the An−1-case we make no distinction between the
parameter: κ = c/t, and the complex parameter ‘k’ entering the deformed Harish-Chandra
homomorphism Φk. Note that the limit κ →∞corresponds to t →0.
For any Weyl group, Cherednik deﬁned a faithful representation of the double aﬃne

Cherednik’s construction yields a faithful representation of Hκ in the vector space C[h
reg].

Calogero-Moser space.
It is a well-known principle that the ‘classical’ limit of the
algebra D(g) is the commutative algebra of regular functions on T ∗g, the cotangent bundle
on g. Since T ∗g = g ⊕g∗≃g ⊕g, the ‘classical’ limit of D(g)g is the algebra C[g ⊕g]g of
ad g-invariant polynomials on g ⊕g. Now, let g = gln. There are reasons to think of the
limit: κ = (t, c) −→∞= (0, 1) ∈P1, as a ‘classical’ limit. Accordingly, we show that
the family of Harish-Chandra homomorphisms Φ
spher
κ
: D(g)g ։ eHκe, see (1.20), has a
‘classical’ limit, a surjective algebra homomorphism Φ
spher
∞
: C[g ⊕g]g ։ eH∞e. It turns
out that this homomorphism is intimately related to the so-called Calogero-Moser space
Mn, a symplectic algebraic manifold introduced by Kazhdan-Kostant-Sternberg [KKS],
and studied further by G. Wilson [Wi]. This space, which is a particular case of the quiver
variety MΓ,n,c, for Γ = {1}, is deﬁned as a categorical quotient: Mn
= M//Ad GLn,
where
M := {(X, Y ) ∈g × g

[X, Y ] + Id = rank 1 matrix} ,
(1.22)
is an aﬃne subvariety in g ⊕g, a special case of the quiver data variety Mc

by deﬁnition, the coordinate ring of Mn equals C[Mn] = C[g ⊕g]g/(IM)g, where IM ⊂
C[g⊕g] denotes the deﬁning ideal of M, and the superscript ‘ g ’ stands for ‘ad g-invariants’.

===== PDF PAGE 12 =====

Theorem 1.24 If W = Sn is the Symmetric group and κ = ∞= (0, c) , c ̸= 0, then
(i) The algebra H∞= H0,c(Sn) is Morita equivalent to the algebra: Z∞≃C[Mn].
(ii) Any simple H∞-module is isomorphic to one of the form:
H∞e ⊗Z∞χ, for a
certain character χ : Z∞→C. Thus, the isomorphism classes of simple H∞-modules are
parametrized by points of the Calogero-Moser space Mn.
(iii) Any simple H∞-module has dimension n! and is isomorphic, as an Sn-module, to
the regular representation of Sn.
Berest and Wilson have introduced an (inﬁnite-dimensional) group G of the automor-
phisms of C⟨x, y⟩, the free associative algebra on two generators, that keep the element
xy −yx ∈C⟨x, y⟩ﬁxed. We show that the group G acts naturally on D(gln) and on
Hκ by algebra automorphisms, preserving subalgebras D(gln)gln and eHκe, respectively.
Moreover, the map Φ
spher
κ
: D(gln)gln −→eHκe commutes with the G-action. Further, in
[BW], Berest-Wilson deﬁned a transitive action of G on the Calogero-Moser space Mn. It
follows from our results that the G-action on Mn can be canonically lifted to a G-action
on the vector bundle R, see Theorem 1.7. It seems to us quite important to obtain a
better geometric understanding of this vector bundle.
Guide for the reader.
Theorem 1.3, Theorem 1.6, and part (i) of Theorem 1.8 are
proved in §2. Theorem 1.5, Theorem 1.7, part (ii) of Theorem 1.8, and Proposition 1.10
are proved in §3. For the proof of the Satake isomorphism see Theorem 3.1, and normality
of Spec Z0,c is Lemma 3.5. Rational Cherednik algebras are discussed in §4. Theorem 1.13
about the structure of Spec Z0,c for wreath-products is a combination of Theorem 11.16,
and Theorem 16.1 (Appendix E). The image and the kernel of the deformed Harish-
Chandra homomorphism Φk are described in Theorem 7.3, which contains in particular
Theorem 1.17 as part of the statement. The proof of Theorem 7.3 is given in §10, but it
depends in an essential way on Theorem 9.1, and on Theorem 6.8, proved in §9 and in
references [LS1],[LS2],[Wa], respectively. Finally, Theorem 1.23 is Theorem 11.29(ii).

PART 1. SYMPLECTIC REFLECTION ALGEBRAS
2
Koszul patterns and deformations
Recall the setup outlined in the Introduction. Thus (V, ω) is a ﬁnite dimensional sym-
plectic vector space, and Γ ⊂Sp(V ) is a ﬁnite group, such that the triple (V, ω, Γ) is
indecomposable, i.e., there is no ω-orthogonal direct sum decomposition: V = V1 ⊕V2,
12


===== PDF PAGE 13 =====

where Vi are Γ-stable proper symplectic vector subspaces in V . Schur lemma implies that
there are exactly two situations in which (V, ω, Γ) is indecomposable:
•
V is an irreducible Γ-module of quaternionic type, i.e. (Λ2V ∗)Γ ̸= 0.
•
V = h ⊕h∗, where h is an irreducible Γ-module of either real or complex type.
An application of Schur lemma shows that a triple (V, ω, Γ) is indecomposable if and only
if the space (Λ2V ∗)Γ is 1-dimensional, in which case this space is spanned by ω.
Given a skew-symmetric C-bilinear pairing κ : V ×V →CΓ, we introduce an associative
algebra Hκ as deﬁned in (1.1).
Proof of Theorem 1.3:
First of all, it is clear that a necessary condition for the PBW-
property is that the form κ is Γ-invariant (where Γ acts on itself by conjugation); otherwise
the PBW property fails already in degree two of the ﬁltration. Thus from now on we will
assume that κ is Γ-invariant.
Write: v 7→vg for the action of g ∈Γ on V . Set K = CΓ, and let E = V ⊗C CΓ.
The space E has a natural K-bimodule structure, with left Γ-action given by g : v ⊗a 7→
vg⊗(ga), and right Γ-action given by g : v⊗a 7→v⊗(ag). Let TKE = L

CV )⊗C CΓ. This gives a canonical graded
algebra isomorphism: TKE ≃(TV )#Γ. Thus we can write: Hκ = TKE/I⟨P⟩, where I⟨P⟩
is the two-sided ideal in TKE generated by a certain K-bimodule P ⊂K ⊕(E ⊗K E).
Explicitly, write E ∧E for the K-subbimodule in E ⊗K E = V ⊗C V ⊗C CΓ spanned by
elements of the form: x ⊗y ⊗g −y ⊗x ⊗g , x, y ∈V, g ∈Γ, and let κK be the (unique)
K-bimodule map: E ∧E →K that extends the pairing κ : V ⊗V →K. Then we have:

We see that Hκ = TKE/I⟨P⟩is a nonhomogeneous quadratic K-algebra, in the ter-

explained e.g. in [BGS, n.2.7], everything works for quadratic algebras over any ground
ring R, provided R is a ﬁnite dimensional semisimple C-algebra, e.g., R = CΓ). Similarly,
we have: SV #Γ ≃TKE/I⟨E ∧E⟩, is a homogeneous quadratic K-algebra. Moreover,
since SV is a Koszul C-algebra, cf. [BGS], it is easy to deduce that SV #Γ is a Koszul
K-algebra. Thus, to prove that the PBW-property holds for Hκ = TKE/I⟨P⟩we may
apply (a ‘K-version’ of) the criterion of [BG]; similar criteria have been obtained earlier
by Drinfeld [Dr1], and Polishchuk-Positselsky [PP]. According to [BG, Thm. 4.1] we must
verify conditions (i)-(iii) of [BG, Lemma 3.3]. In our situation, conditions (i) and (iii) of
that lemma become vacuous, and condition (ii) reads:

x, y 7→b(s, x, y). Hence, this form is proportional to ωs, since any two 2-forms on the
two-dimensional vector space Im(Id −s) must be proportional.

Remark.
Deﬁne an oriented quiver Q whose vertex set I is the set of (isomorphism
classes of) simple Γ-modules. Given two vertices, i.e. two simple Γ-modules ρ, σ ∈I, we
let dim HomΓ(ρ ⊗V, σ) be the number of arrows in Q with head ρ and tail σ. Note that
since V is a self-dual Γ-module, the number of arrows from ρ to σ equals the number of
arrows from σ to ρ. One can show, mimicking the argument in [CBH, §3], that the algebra
Ht,c is Morita equivalent to a proper quotient of a suitable deformed preprojective algebra
associated to Q (viewed as the double of some other quiver), see [CBH]. For a general group
Γ ⊂Sp(V ), the quiver Q is quite complicated, as illustrated by the following example.
Let Γ = Sn be the Symmetric group acting on V = Cn−1 ⊕Cn−1, a sum of two copies
of the permutation representation in Cn−1 = {(x1, . . . , xn) ∈Cn  P xi = 0} . Then one

where N is the number of (outer) corners in the Young diagram corresponding to ρ,
and two representations are said to be adjacent if the corresponding Young diagrams are
obtained from each other by moving one corner box from one place to another.

Koszul complex for H has ﬁnite length, there are only ﬁnitely many non-zero cohomology
groups involved. Thus, the map (2.14) is a map between C[r]-modules of the same generic
rank.

specializes to a bijection. It follows that there is at most a countable set BAD ⊂C such

Observe that the family {At}t∈C, where At := A
Γ
t if t ̸= 0, and At=0 := (SV )Γ, is
ﬂat, hence, gives a deformation of the algebra At=0 = (SV )Γ. Since the latter algebra
is commutative, the general construction of §15 yields a Poisson structure on the algebra
(SV )Γ = C[V ∗]Γ.
It is well-known and easy to verify that the corresponding Poisson
bracket {−, −}ω is obtained by restricting the Poisson bracket on C[V ∗] induced by the
symplectic structure on V ∗. Note that, for the standard grading (SV )Γ = L
i≥0 (SiV )Γ,
we have {−, −}ω : (SiV )Γ × (SjV )Γ −→(Si+j−2V )Γ. More generally, a Poisson bracket
B on (SV )Γ is said to be of degree ℓif, for any i, j ≥0, we have B : (SiV )Γ × (SjV )Γ −→
(Si+j+ℓV )Γ.
20


===== PDF PAGE 21 =====

Lemma 2.23 (i) Any Poisson bracket on (SV )Γ of degree (−2) is proportional to {−, −}ω.
(ii) Any Poisson bracket on (SV )Γ of degree ℓ< −2 is zero.
Proof. We identify V with V ∗using the symplectic form. Let Y be the set of points
of V with a nontrivial isotropy group in Γ. Then (V ∖Y )/Γ is a smooth manifold. A
Poisson bracket on (SV )Γ in particular deﬁnes a Poisson bracket on (V ∖Y )/Γ, which is
the same as a Γ-invariant Poisson bracket, that is a bivector ﬁeld, on V ∖Y .
Since Γ ⊂Sp(V ) is a ﬁnite group, any nontrivial element of Γ has at least two eigenval-
ues ̸= 1. This implies that the codimension of Y is at least 2. Thus, by Hartogs theorem,
any regular bivector on V ∖Y extends by continuity to a regular algebraic bivector on the
whole of V . But a regular bivector on V of homogeneity degree ℓmust vanish if ℓ< −2,
and comes from an element of Λ2V if ℓ= −2. In the latter case, since our bracket is
Γ-invariant, by indecomposability of (V, ω, Γ), this bracket must be a multiple of {−, −}ω.
□
Lemma 2.24 Let t, c be such that the algebra Ht,c is commutative. Let χ : eHt,ce →C be
a generic character, and let Tχ = Ht,ce⊗eHt,ce χ be the corresponding induced Ht,c-module.
Then Tχ is isomorphic, as a Γ-module, to the regular representation of Γ.
Proof. Consider the family of algebras {Hrt,rc}r∈C. These algebras are isomorphic to
each other for all r ̸= 0, so it is suﬃcient to prove the lemma for a generic member of
the family. To this end, we treat the family {Hrt,rc}r∈C as a single C[r]-algebra eH. This
algebra is ﬂat over C[r], due to PBW-property. It follows that eeHe is a commutative ﬂat
C[r]-algebra.
Further, eHe is a ﬁnitely generated eeHe-module, due to Theorem 1.5(ii) to be proved
below (which is independent of the material in between). Fix a simple Γ-module E, and
set F = HomΓ(E, eHe).
By standard results in invariant theory, see proof of Theorem
1.5 below, F is a ﬁnitely generated eeHe-module which is, moreover, ﬂat over over C[r],
by the PBW-property. We write Fr = F/r·F = HomΓ(E, Hrt,rce) for the corresponding
eHrt,rce-module.
Given a commutative C-algebra A without zero-divisors, and a A-module M, let
rkA(M) denote the generic rank of M, i.e., rkA(M) := dimQ(A)
 Q(A) ⊗A M

, where
Q(A) stands for the ﬁeld of fractions of A. It is clear that rkeH0,0e(F0) = dim E. On the
other hand, proving the lemma amounts to showing that rk
eeHeF = dim E.
To this end, let p = (eeHe) · r ⊂eeHe be the prime ideal generated by r, and set
A := (eeHe)(p), the localization of eeHe with respect to p. Thus, A is a local ring with
the residue class ﬁeld A0 := A/A·r ≃Q(eH0,0e), the ﬁeld of fractions of the algebra
eH0,0e. Furthermore, A is a local ring of dimension 1 with local parameter r, due to the
C[r]-ﬂatness of eeHe. Let F = A ⊗
eeHe F denote the A-module obtained by localizing at p
the eeHe-module F, and set F0 := F/F·r, an A0-module. Observe that since F is C[r]-ﬂat,
and r is a local parameter on A, the module F is free over A. Thus, we obtain
rk
eeHeF = rkAF = rkA0(F0) = rkeH0,0 e(F0) .

Proof of Theorem 1.6. Introduce an auxiliary variable ‘ℏ’, and set TV [ℏ] = TV ⊗C[ℏ].
We treat TV [ℏ] as a graded algebra with deg ℏ= 2, and assume that the group Γ acts
trivially on ℏ. Let
eH := (TV [ℏ]#Γ)/I⟨x ⊗y −y ⊗x −κ(x, y) · ℏ∈T 2V ⊕CΓ[ℏ]⟩x,y∈V ,
be a ‘homogenized’ version of the algebra Hκ. We set A = eeHe, the corresponding spherical
subalgebra. Clearly, A is a ﬂat C[ℏ]-algebra such that: A/ℏA = gr(eHt,ce). Moreover,
since A/(ℏ−1)A = eHt,ce, one may view the algebra eHt,ce as a ﬂat ℏ-deformation
of the commutative algebra gr(eHt,ce) = (SV )Γ.
Thus, for any (t, c), by the general
construction of §15, this deformation gives rise to a Poisson bracket Bt,c on (SV )Γ. Let
mt,c ∈{1, 2, . . . , ∞}, be the number involved in the construction of the Poisson bracket,
see Lemma 15.1. We have:
Claim 2.25 (i) There are only two alternatives: either
mt,c = 1, or
mt,c = ∞. The
algebra eHt,ce is non-commutative if mt,c = 1, and commutative if mt,c = ∞.
(ii) If mt,c = 1, then Bt,c = f(t, c) · {−, −}ω, where f : C ⊕C →C is a non-zero linear
function.
To prove part (i) of the Claim, note that since deg ℏ= 2, it is clear that the Poisson
bracket Bt,c has degree (−2mt,c). Thus, by Lemma 2.23(ii), in order for Bt,c to be non-zero
we must have mt,c = 1. But, by Lemma 15.1, vanishing of Bt,c implies commutativity of
the algebra A, and (i) follows.
To prove part (ii) of Claim 2.25, we change our point of view, and regard t and c as
variables, and assign them grade degree 2, while set deg ℏ= 0. Thus, A becomes now
a graded C[C ⊕C]-algebra, depending on ℏ, the deformation parameter. Applying the
Poisson bracket construction of §15 in this new setting we get, for m = 1, a bracket B
on (SV )Γ N C[C ⊕C] of degree (−2). By Lemma 2.23(i), we have: B = f(t, c){−, −}ω,
for some f(t, c) ∈C. Since the relations in Ht,c become homogeneous in our new grading:
deg(t) = deg(c) = 2, it follows that: t, c 7→f(t, c) is a certain linear function on C ⊕C.
Moreover, for any (t, c) ∈C ⊕C such that Bt,c ̸= 0, the Poisson bracket Bt,c is clearly a
specialisation of B at the point (t, c), that is: Bt,c = f(t, c){−, −}ω. This completes the
proof of Claim 2.25(ii).
Claim 2.25 implies that the algebra eHt,ce is commutative if and only if f(t, c) = 0.
We see that the parameters (t, c) such that eHt,ce is commutative form a hyper-plane in
C⊕C given by the equation: f(t, c) = 0. Therefore, to complete the proof of the theorem,
it suﬃces to show that f(t, c) is a multiple of t, i.e. that the above hyper-plane is the one
given by the equation t = 0. To prove this assume that, for some t, c, one has f(t, c) = 0,
and hence the algebra eHt,ce is commutative.
In this case, choose a generic character
χ : eHt,ce →C, and let Tχ = Ht,c ⊗eHt,ce χ be the corresponding induced Ht,c-module. By
lemma 2.24, this module is isomorphic, as a Γ module, to the regular representation of Γ.
Now, for any g ∈Γ such that g ̸= 1, in the regular Γ-representation one has tr(g) = 0.
Taking the traces in Tχ of both sides of the main commutation relation: x ⊗y −y ⊗x =
22


===== PDF PAGE 23 =====

κ(x, y) in the algebra Ht,c we deduce: t = 0. Thus, the two hyperplanes: f(t, c) = 0, and
t = 0 coincide, and we are done.
□
According to Theorem 1.6, the algebra eH0,ce is commutative for any c ∈C, and the
family {eHt,ce}t∈C gives, by the PBW-theorem, a ﬂat deformation of eH0,ce. Hence, by
the general construction of §15 this deformation induces a Poisson bracket on eH0,ce, to be
denoted {−, −}. Recall further that the algebra eH0,ce has a natural increasing ﬁltration
F•(eH0,ce) induced from that on H0,c.

Lemma 2.26 Let B be a non-zero Poisson bracket on eH0,ce of ﬁltration degree ℓ≤−2.
Then ℓ= −2 and, moreover, B = λ · {−, −}, for some non-zero constant λ ∈C.
Proof. The Poisson bracket B induces naturally a non-zero Poisson bracket gr B on
gr(eH0,ce) of degree ℓ. Recall that gr(eH0,ce) ≃(SV )Γ. Thus, we may view gr B as a non-
zero Poisson bracket on (SV )Γ. Hence, Lemma 2.23(ii) yields: ℓ= −2. Moreover, part (i)
of the same Lemma implies that there exists a constant λ ∈C such that gr B = λ·{−, −}ω.
It follows that B −λ · {−, −} is a Poisson bracket on eH0,ce of ﬁltration degree < ℓ= −2.
The argument above shows that this bracket must vanish, and we are done.

3
Representation theory of the algebra H0,c
Proof of Theorem 1.5:
It is known from invariant theory that (SV )Γ is a ﬁnitely
generated algebra and, moreover, SV is a ﬁnitely generated (SV )Γ-module: by Hilbert’s
Basis theorem, each isotypic component of SV is ﬁnitely generated over (SV )Γ, see e.g.
[PV]. Hence, the isomorphisms: gr
 eHt,ce

≃(SV )Γ and gr
 Ht,ce

≃SV
insured by
Theorem 1.3 imply that eHt,ce is a ﬁnitely generated algebra without zero divisors, and
Ht,ce is a ﬁnitely generated eHt,ce-module.
Next we need to recall some deﬁnitions. Given a not necessarily commutative asso-
ciative C-algebra A, one deﬁnes the notion of a rigid Auslander dualizing complex for A,
see [VB], [YZ]. This is an object DA ∈Df(A-bimod) = bounded derived category of com-
plexes of A-bimodules, whose cohomology groups are ﬁnitely generated both as left and
as right A-modules. Such a dualizing complex might or might not exist, in general, but
if it exists it is unique [VB], and enjoys nice functorial properties, as explained in [YZ].
Furthermore, it has been shown in [VB], [YZ] that DA does exist if A is a positively ﬁltered
C-algebra, such that gr(A) is a ﬁnitely generated commutative algebra with gr0(A) = C.
In particular, for a commutative ﬁnitely generated algebra A, the dualizing complex, DA,
exists and coincides, due to the uniqueness property, with the one known in Commutative
Algebra.
23


===== PDF PAGE 24 =====

Fix an algebra A that has the rigid Auslander dualizing complex, DA. We say that
A is Cohen-Macaulay if all the cohomology groups Hi(DA) vanish, except for i = 0. In
the situation of a ﬁltered algebra A, as above, our deﬁnition agrees, by [YZ, Prop. 6.18],
with other deﬁnitions of (non-commutative) Cohen-Macaulay rings that involve Gelfand-
Kirillov dimensions, e.g., those used in [Bj] and [CBH]. For a Cohen-Macaulay algebra A,
we will abuse the notation, and write DA for the bimodule H0(DA), quasi-isomorphic to it.
A Cohen-Macaulay algebra A is called Gorenstein if DA = A. Further, a ﬁnitely generated
(left) module M over a Cohen-Macaulay algebra A is said to be a Cohen-Macaulay module
of degree d if, for all i ̸= d we have: Exti
A-left(M, DA) = 0. For commutative algebras, all
these deﬁnitions agree with the standard ones.
The commutative algebra gr

Observe further that dimC(FmEnd) < ∞, for any m ∈Z, and if d =

 EndeHκe(Hκe)

where j is the canonical map, which is clearly injective.
It suﬃces to prove that the
composite map j ◦gr(η) is bijective. The injectivity is obvious, since j ◦gr(η) becomes an
isomorphism after tensoring with C(V ∗)Γ, the ﬁeld of Γ-invariant rational functions on
V ∗. To prove the surjectivity, we need to show that any endomorphism of SV over (SV )Γ
comes from an element of SV #Γ.
Let a : SV →SV be an (SV )Γ-linear map. Then it deﬁnes a C(V ∗)Γ-linear map
C(V ∗) →C(V ∗). So a = P ag · g, where ag ∈C(V ∗), g ∈Γ. It is clear that the functions
ag must be regular on V ∖Y , where Y is the set of points with a nontrivial isotropy group
in Γ (since on V ∖Y , the surjectivity is obvious). But Y has codimension ≥2, so by
Hartogs theorem ag are regular, as desired.
To prove (iii), we consider the multiplication pairing: eHκ ⊗Hκe −→eHκe. This
pairing induces a right eHκe-linear map ψ : eHκ →HomeHκe(Hκe , eHκe), given by
ψ(x)(y) = xy. This map is injective by part (iv), since ψ is the restriction of η to eHκ.
It is also surjective. Indeed, by part (iv), for any a ∈(Hκe)∨, there exists x ∈Hκ such
that xy = a(y) for all y ∈Hκe. But then we also have exy = a(y), since a(y) ∈eHκe. So
a = ψ(ex). Hence, ψ is an isomorphism, and the ﬁrst statement is proved. The second
statement of part (iii) follows similarly. The theorem is proved.
□
Remark.
There is an alternative proof of the isomorphisms in parts (ii) and (iii) of
Theorem 1.5, along the same lines as the proof of Lemmas 1.3, 1.4 in [CBH]. That proof
is less elementary and involves a lot of non-commutative algebra machinery.
♦
Note that the algebra Ht,0 is isomorphic to At#Γ, provided t ̸= 0. The latter is known,
see e.g. [Mo], to be a simple algebra with trivial center. It follows by semi-continuity that,
for generic values (t, c) ∈C, the algebra Ht,c also has the trivial center. We will see below
that the points (0, c) are very special: the algebra H0,c has a large center, Z0,c := Z(H0,c).
Further, for any c ∈C, the construction of Hayashi [Ha], cf. §15, applied to the ﬂat
family {Ht,c}t∈C , gives a Poisson algebra structure on Z0,c.
Theorem 3.1 (Satake isomorphism) For any c ∈C, the map: Z0,c →eH0,ce , z 7→
z · e, is a Poisson algebra isomorphism.
Proof. It is clear that the map: z 7→z·e gives an algebra homomorphism ζ : Z(H0,c) →
eH0,ce. Since the algebra eH0,ce is commutative, we can also construct an algebra homo-
morphism ξ : eH0,ce →Z(H0,c) as follows. For any, a ∈eH0,ce, right multiplication by
a gives, due to commutativity of eH0,ce, a right eH0,ce-linear endomorphism of H0,ce.
This endomorphism must arise from the left action of an element ξ(a) ∈H0,c, since
EndeH0,ce(H0,ce) = H0,c, by Theorem 1.5(ii). Moreover, the element ξ(a) belongs to the
center of H0,c since the left ξ(a)-action on H0,ce, being induced by multiplication on the
right, commutes with the left action on H0,ce of any other element of H0,c. It is clear that
25


===== PDF PAGE 26 =====

the assignment: a 7→ξ(a) gives an algebra homomorphism ξ : eH0,ce →Z(H0,c), and that
this homomorphism is inverse to ζ.
The compatibility of the map: z 7→z · e with the Poisson brackets follows directly
from the ”deformation-construction” of the brackets on eH0,ce and on Z(H0,c) given in
§15, once we know that the integer m0,c involved in the construction of the bracket on
eH0,ce equals 1, cf. Lemma 15.1. But this has been established in the course of the proof
of Theorem 1.6.
□
Remark.
The isomorphism of Theorem 3.1 is analogous to the Satake isomorphism
between the center and the spherical subalgebra of the aﬃne Hecke algebra, due to
Lusztig [Lu].

Proof of theorem 1.7:
Recall that U is a smooth Zariski open dense subset in Spec Z0,c,
and RU denotes the coherent sheaf on U corresponding to HU e, viewed as a (ﬁnitely
generated) ZU-module, so that Γ(U, RU ) = HU e. The left HU -action on HU e gives an
HU -module structure on each geometric ﬁber of RU .
Proving that the sheaf RU is locally free amounts to showing that HU e is a ﬁnite rank
projective ZU-module. To this end, recall the following standard result of commutative
algebra. Let A be a ﬁnitely generated commutative C-algebra with unit, such that Spec A
is a smooth algebraic variety. Then, a ﬁnitely-generated A-module M is Cohen-Macaulay
if and only if it is projective, see [Se, Ch.4, Cor.2].
Since U is smooth by our assumptions, it follows from Theorem 1.5(ii) and Satake
isomorphism that HUe is a ﬁnite rank projective ZU-module. Part (i) now follows from
Theorem 1.5(vi).
Further, the endomorphism algebra of an algebraic vector bundle on an aﬃne algebraic
variety is always Morita equivalent to the coordinate ring on this variety. Explicitly, in
our case, the equivalence is provided by the following mutually inverse functors
HU -mod

ZU-mod
,
F(M) = HU e ⊗ZU M
,
G(N) = eHU ⊗HU N = e·N .
To prove (vi), notice that since RU is a vector bundle it is suﬃcient, due to rigid-
ity of Γ-modules, to establish that the generic ﬁber of RU is isomorphic to the regular
representation of Γ. But this follows from Lemma 2.24.
□
Remarks.
(i) We have established a special case of the following well-known general
result: If A is an associative algebra and e ∈A an idempotent, then A is Morita equivalent
to eAe if and only if we have: AeA = A.
(ii) We have shown that each geometric ﬁber of R over the regular locus of Spec Z0,c
is isomorphic, as a CΓ-module, to the regular representation of Γ.
Proof of Proposition 1.10.
Given an associative algebra A, one has, see [Lo], a Chern
character map ch : K(A) →HCev(A), where K(A) stands for the Grothendieck group of
ﬁnitely generated projective A-modules, and HCev(A) denotes the even degree part of the
periodic cyclic homology groups of A, see [Lo]. The Chern character map is compatible
with Morita equivalences. It is also known, see [Lo], that if A = C[X] is the coordinate
ring of a smooth aﬃne algebraic variety, then: HCev(A) ≃L
i H2i(X), the direct sum of
even cohomology of X, as a topological space. Moreover, the map ch reduces in this case
to the classical Chern character map: K(X) →L
i H2i(X).
Recall now that the algebra H0,c has a canonical increasing ﬁltration, F•(H0,c), such
that F0(H0,c) = CΓ.

Lemma 3.5 (i) For any c ∈C, the variety Spec Z0,c is normal.
(ii) If the variety Spec Z0,c is smooth, then the Poisson structure on Z0,c is non-
degenerate, i.e., Spec Z0,c is a symplectic manifold.
Proof. Note that, for c = 0, the singular locus of Spec Z0,0 = V/Γ has codimension
≥2 in Spec Z0,0.
The codimension of the singular locus being semi-continuous under
going to the asymptotic cone it follows that, for any c ∈C, the singular locus of Spec Z0,c
has codimension ≥2 in Spec Z0,c. Further, we know that Spec Z0,c is a Cohen-Macaulay
variety, by Theorem 1.5(i).
But any Cohen-Macaulay variety which is non-singular in
codimension one is normal, see e.g. proof in [CG, §2.2]. Part (i) follows.
To prove (ii), assume Spec Z0,c is smooth, write βc for the bivector on Spec Z0,c giving
the Poisson structure, and set 2d = dim V = dim(Spec Z0,c). The set of points where the
Poisson structure degenerates equals the set of points where the 2d-vector ∧dβc vanishes.
This set is clearly a divisor (a codimension one closed subvariety) in Spec Z0,c. Let J ⊂
Z0,c denote the deﬁning ideal of that subvariety, and gr(J) ⊂gr(Z0,c) = C[V ]Γ, the
corresponding associated graded ideal with respect to the canonical ﬁltration on H0,c. Then
gr(J) is a proper non-zero ideal in C[V ]Γ, and the zero-variety of gr(J) is a codimension
one subvariety in V/Γ, the ‘asymptotic cone’ to the zero-set of ∧dβc. It is clear that ∧dβ0
is the leading homogeneous component of ∧dβc, hence, vanishes on this asymptotic cone.
Hence, the Poisson structure on V/Γ must degenerate at a divisor. On the other hand, the
Poisson structure on V/Γ is non-degenerate at any smooth point, and the singular locus
of V/Γ has codimension ≥2 in V/Γ. The contradiction completes the proof.
□
For any c ∈C, let RepCΓ(H0,c) be the variety of all algebra homomorphisms: H0,c −→
EndC(CΓ), whose restriction to CΓ is the CΓ-action by left multiplication.
This is an
aﬃne algebraic variety. Given an H0,c-module M ∈RepCΓ(H0,c), the space eM is clearly
a line which is stable under the action of Z0,c. The action on this line gives an algebra
homomorphism χM : Z0,c →C. It is clear that the assignment: M 7→χM gives a morphism
of algebraic varieties,
π : RepCΓ(H0,c) −→Spec Z0,c .
(3.6)

Let AutΓ(CΓ) be the group of C-linear automorphisms of the vector space CΓ that

Theorem 3.7 (i) There is a unique irreducible component, Rep◦
CΓ(H0,c), of the vari-

Proof. It follows from theorem 1.7 that any point of RepCΓ(H0,c) containing, as a subquo-

to a regular function on the whole of Spec Z0,c, due to normality of the latter, see Lemma
3.5(i). Hence, ψ is represented by an element of Z0,c, i.e. there is z ∈Z0,c such that the
functions f and π∗(z) coincide on an open dense subset of Rep◦
CΓ(H0,c). Thus, f = π∗(z)
on Rep◦
CΓ(H0,c), and we are done.

Given an associative algebra A, we write [A, A] for the C-vector subspace in A spanned
by the commutators: [a, b]a,b∈A . Here is a useful criterion for the smoothness of Spec Z0,c.
Proposition 3.8 For any c ∈C, and a Zariski open aﬃne subset U ⊂Spec Z0,c, the
following conditions are equivalent:
(i) The dimension of any simple HU-module is ≥|Γ|.
(ii) U is a smooth algebraic variety.
(iii) For any g ∈Γ , g ̸= 1, we have: g ∈[HU , HU ].
(iv) For any simple HU -module M, and any g ∈Γ , g ̸= 1, we have: Tr(g, M) = 0.
In particular, if any of the conditions (i), (iii) or (vi) holds for U = Spec Z0,c, then Spec Z0,c
is smooth.
We refer the reader to §16 for some applications of Proposition 3.8.
Remark.
It will be clear from the proof of the Proposition that conditions (i)-(vi)
imply automatically that the restriction to Γ of any simple HU -module is isomorphic to
the regular representation of Γ.
♦

(but not on CΓ) factors throught the group PAutΓ(CΓ). We deﬁne a canonical splitting:
PAutΓ(CΓ) ֒→AutΓ(CΓ) of the natural projection by the requirement, that the image
of PAutΓ(CΓ) in AutΓ(CΓ) acts trivially on the element e ∈CΓ. This splitting makes it
possible to view the bundle: CΓ × Rep◦

CΓ(H0,c) as a PAutΓ(CΓ)-equivariant
H0,c-vector bundle.

with U, hence view eRU as a locally free coherent sheaf on U. The (constant) section of
bRU corresponding to the element e ∈CΓ gives rise to a non-vanishing regular section
of eRU , to be denoted e.
Using the HU -action on eRU, we deﬁne an HU -module map:
HU e −→Γ(U, eR) by the assignment: u·e 7→u·e. This gives an HU-equivariant morphism
ǫ : RU →eRU of coherent sheaves on U. The morphism ǫ is clearly an isomorphism over
the generic point of U, hence is injective since the sheaf RU, being reﬂexive, is torsion
free. On the other hand, for any χ ∈U, the induced map on the ﬁbers: Rχ →eRχ is a
non-zero map of HU -modules, hence is surjective, since eRχ is a simple HU -module by our
hypothesis (i). Therefore, ǫ is an isomorphism, by Nakayama lemma. Thus, the sheaf RU
is also locally free.
Recall that, according to a well known criterion, a ﬁnitely generated commutative
algebra A is smooth (i.e. the scheme Spec A is smooth) if and only if A has ﬁnite (global)
homological dimension. Thus, U is smooth if and only if ZU has ﬁnite (global) homological
dimension. On the other hand, since SV is smooth, the algebra gr(H0,c) ≃SV #Γ, clearly
30


===== PDF PAGE 31 =====

has ﬁnite homological dimension. It follows that both the algebra H0,c and HU have ﬁnite
homological dimension. Further, by Theorem 1.7 we have: HU ≃EndZU RU. It follows
that, if RU is a vector bundle, then the algebra HU is Morita equivalent to ZU. Hence the
algebras ZU and HU have equal global homological dimensions, and (ii) follows.
(ii) =⇒(iii) Set Z = ZU. We know that HU ≃EndZRU , by Theorem 1.7. Note that
since any element of [EndZRU, EndZRU ] acts by a trace zero operator in each ﬁber of RU, we

∼

It follows that there is a direct sum decomposition:

M
[HU , HU ].
Now ﬁx g ∈Γ , g ̸= 1. Using the decomposition above we can write: g = z + u, where
z ∈ZU, and u ∈[HU , HU ]. Since the trace of g in the regular representation vanishes, the
element g = z + u acts by a trace zero operator in each ﬁber of RU, by Theorem 1.7(vi).
It follows that z=0. Thus, g = u ∈[HU , HU ].
The implication (iii) =⇒(iv) is clear.

It follows from

Remark.
There is an alternative proof of the Morita equivalence in the implication
(i) =⇒(ii) of the Proposition above, based on a general result of M. Artin (1968) which
in the special situation we need reads:
Let A be a C-algebra with center Z, ﬁnitely generated as a Z-module, and such that:
•
Z has no zero divisors;
•
A injects into A ⊗Z Q(Z), where Q(Z) is the ﬁeld of fractions of Z;
•
Any irreducible A-module has dimension N;
•
A ⊗Z Q(Z) = MatN
 Q(Z)

.
Then A is Morita equivalent to Z.
□
The proof of this result makes a nontrivial use of the theory of polynomial identities.

understanding those associated with groups Γ generated by symplectic reﬂections.
Verbitsky has shown in [Ve] that a necessary condition for the existence of a smooth
symplectic resolution of singularities p : d

by symplectic reﬂections.
We have an analogue of that result for varieties Spec Z0,c.
Speciﬁcally, given any ﬁnite group Γ ⊂Sp(V ), ﬁx c ∈C and let H0,c be the corresponding
symplectic reﬂection algebra. Let Γ◦⊂Γ be the subgroup generated by those symplectic
reﬂections s ∈S for which cs ̸= 0.

The rational Cherednik algebra
Let h be a ﬁnite dimensional complex vector space with a nondegenerate inner product,
and W a ﬁnite group of orthogonal transformations of h generated by reﬂections. For
any reﬂection s ∈W, ﬁx a nonzero linear function αs ∈h∗uniquely determined, up to
a nonzero scalar factor, by the condition: s(αs) = −αs. We choose the scalar factors
in such a way that the function: s 7→α2
s is W-invariant. Write R for the collection of
linear functions {±αs}s=reﬂection in W. For α ∈R ⊂h∗, we let sα denote the corresponding
reﬂection s, and set α∨:= 2(α,−)
(α,α) ∈h (we note that (α, α) ̸= 0 since C · α and α⊥are
eigenspaces of s with diﬀerent eigenvalues, and therefore have zero intersection). A point
x ∈h is called regular if, for all α ∈R, we have: α(x) ̸= 0.
From now on, we ﬁx a complex-valued W-invariant function c : R →C, α 7→cα, and
t ∈C. Deﬁne a rational Cherednik algebra with parameters (t, c) to be the symplectic
reﬂection algebra corresponding to the W-diagonal action on h ⊕h∗. Explicitly, it is an
associative C-algebra Ht,c generated by the spaces h, h∗, and the group W, with deﬁning
relations (1.15).
Example: Sn-case.
In the special case of the root system R = An−1 in the vector
space h = Cn (not in Cn−1) we have: W = Sn. Use the standard coordinates on Cn to
write C[h] = C[x1, . . . , xn] and C[h∗] = C[y1, . . . , yn]. Recall that, in the An−1-case, all
roots are W-conjugate and the function c : R →C reduces to a constant; we will assume
that c = 1, and will view the parameter κ = (t, 1) as a point of P1. Thus, the case t = 0
corresponds to the point κ = ∞.
Write sij ∈Sn for the transposition: i ←→j. The algebra Ht = Ht,1(Sn) has generators
x1, ..., xn, y1, ..., yn and the group Sn, with the following deﬁning relations, which are a

specialization of 1.15:

Proposition 4.9 Suppose that W is a Weyl group with no factors of type E or F. Then
part (i) of Theorem 4.8, in particular, the conclusion of part (ii), holds for all values of t.
Proof. We need to show that the image of Θspher

as a Poisson algebra, by the subalgebras C[h]W and C[h∗]W . But this is shown in [Wa] for
Weyl groups containing no factors of type E or F. □
We expect, but cannot show, that the same result holds for all ﬁnite reﬂection groups.
Relation of Hκ to the double-aﬃne Hecke algebra.
The algebra Hκ is a rational
version of the double-aﬃne Hecke algebra. By this we mean that if W is the Weyl group
associated to a ﬁnite root system R, then Hκ is obtained from the double aﬃne Hecke
algebra attached to W by a certain limiting procedure.
36


===== PDF PAGE 37 =====

To illustrate this, we will consider the special case of the root system of type A1. In
this case W = {1, s}. Thus, if t = 1 and c = cα, for the unique positive root α, then, the
algebra H1,c is generated by 3 elements: s, x, y, with deﬁning relations
s2 = 1
,
sx = −xs
,
sy = −ys
,
[y, x] = 1 −2c · s.
Let us show how the algebra H1,c is obtained from the double-aﬃne Hecke algebra by
a limiting procedure. Let us consider the version of H1,c over C[c], which we will denote
in the same way. Recall that the double-aﬃne Hecke algebra H is the C[q±1, τ ±1]-algebra
with 3 generators: T, X, Y (where X, Y are invertible), and deﬁning relations:
(T −τ)(T + τ −1) = 0 , TXT = X−1 , T −1Y T −1 = Y −1 , Y −1X−1Y XT 2 = q .
Deﬁne a completed double-aﬃne Hecke algebra bH to be a C[c][[h]]-algebra topologically
generated (in the h-adic topology) by s, x, y, with the same deﬁning relations, for X = ehx,
Y = ehy, T = seh2cs, q = eh2, τ = eh2c. It is clear that bH is a certain completion of H.
The proposition below says that the algebra bH is a ﬂat deformation of H1,c.
Proposition 4.10 The algebra bH is ﬂat over C[[h]], and bH/h · bH = H1,c.
Proof. The last statement is obtained directly from the relations: one shows that the
relations of bH are deformations of the relations of H1,c. The ﬂatness statement follows
from the fact, due to Cherednik, that the representation of H1,c by Dunkl operators can be
deformed to a faithful representation of bH by q-diﬀerence analogs of Dunkl operators.
□
A similar relation between Hκ and the double-aﬃne Hecke algebra exists for any Weyl
group. The statement and proof of this fact is analogous to the sl2 case.
Fourier transform.
There is an important algebra automorphism F : Hκ →Hκ, to be
called the Fourier transform. The automorphism F is a rational version of Cherednik’s
“diﬀerence Fourier transform” for double aﬃne Hecke algebras. In particular, in the sl2-
case, it is the classical Fourier-Hankel transform, see [ChM].
Let (−, −) be the W-invariant bilinear form on h that has been ﬁxed throughout. For
x ∈h∗, let ˇx be the element of h such that x = (ˇx, −), and for y ∈h, let ˇy = (y, −) be
the corresponding element of h∗. The Fourier transform on Hκ is deﬁned to be an algebra
homomorphism F : Hκ →Hκ given on generators by the following assignment:
F : x 7→ˇx
,
y 7→−ˇy
,
w 7→w,
∀x ∈h∗, y ∈h , w ∈W.
It is clear that F extends to an automorphism of Hκ preserving the subalgebra eHκe. It
follows that (in cases when eHκe is isomorphic to Bκ, see Theorem 4.8 and Proposition
4.9) it also acts on Bκ, permuting C[h]W and Cκ. In particular, this is so for type An.

Since gr(Z0,c) = Z0,0 is a ﬁnitely generated algebra without zero divisors, it follows
that Z0,c is a ﬁnitely generated algebra without zero divisors as well. Therefore, it deﬁnes
an irreducible aﬃne algebraic variety Spec Z0,c of dimension equal to dim V . This variety
has a canonical Poisson structure. Thus, Spec Z0,c is a Poisson deformation of the Poisson
variety V ∗/W, cf. also §17. We propose the following
Deﬁnition 4.14 The Poisson variety Spec Z(H0,c) is called the Calogero-Moser space as-
sociated to W, and the parameter c ∈C.
The variety Spec Z(H0,c) may be regarded as the “correct” completion of the phase
space for the classical Calogero-Moser system acssociated to the Weyl group W. We have
shown that Spec Z(H0,c) is an irreducible Gorenstein normal variety.
The problem of
smoothness of Spec Z(H0,c) will be considered, in a few special cases, in Appendix E.
We now study the center of the algebra H0,c in some detail.
Proposition 4.15 For an arbitrary ﬁnite complex reﬂection group W, we have:
(i) The subalgebras C[h∗]W ⊂H0,c and C[h]W ⊂H0,c are both contained in Z0,c.
(ii) The algebra Z0,c is a free C[h]W ⊗C[h∗]W -module of rank |W|.
Proof. The inclusion: C[h∗]W ⊂Z0,c follows from the injectivity of Θ0,c and the fact
that, for f ∈C[h∗]W , we have Θ0,c(f) ∈C[h
reg]W , which is obviously in the center of
C[h
reg × h∗]#W. The inclusion: C[h]W ⊂Z0,c follows from the previous one by applying
the Fourier automorphism F. Thus, we have shown that: C[h]W ⊗C[h∗]W ⊂Z0,c.
Further, C[h ⊕h∗] is a free module over its subalgebra C[h]W ⊗C[h∗]W ⊂C[h ⊕h∗],
by the Chevalley theorem. Moreover, one has a W × W-module isomorphism:
C[h ⊕h∗] ≃C[W × W]

.
Taking the invariants with respect to the diagonal subgroup W ⊂W × W shows that
C[h ⊕h∗]W is a free C[h]W ⊗C[h∗]W -module of rank |W|. Part (ii) now follows from the
isomorphism gr(Z0,c)
∼

The imbedding: C[h]W ⊗C[h∗]W

surjective map Υ : Spec Z0,c ։ h/W × h∗/W, of degree |W|. Let |W/Ad | denote the
number of conjugacy classes in the ﬁnite complex reﬂection group W.
Proposition 4.16 If Spec Z0,c is smooth, then the ﬁber Υ−1(0) ⊂Spec Z0,c consists of
|W/Ad | points.
39


===== PDF PAGE 40 =====

Proof. The C∗-action on h ⊕h∗given by the assignment C∗∋λ : y ⊕x 7→λ·y ⊕λ−1·x
extends to a C∗-action on Ht,c by algebra automorphisms keeping the group W pointwise
ﬁxed (this is part of the SL2(C)-action on Ht,c considered in Corollary 5.3). The C∗-action
on H0,c induces an action on Z0,c, hence on Spec Z0,c.
Furthermore, the C∗-action on h ⊕h∗induces one on h/W × h∗/W, so that the map
Υ becomes C∗-equivariant. Observe that the point (0, 0) ∈h/W × h∗/W is the only ﬁxed
point of the C∗-action on h/W × h∗/W. It follows, since C∗is connected and the ﬁber
Υ−1(0) is ﬁnite, that each point of Υ−1(0) is a C∗-ﬁxed point, and these are the only ﬁxed
points on Spec Z0,c.
Now, the Lefschetz ﬁxed point formula for the C∗-action on Spec Z0,c yields: |Υ−1(0)| =
χ(Spec Z0,c), where χ(−) stands for the Euler characteristic.
On the other hand, if
Spec Z0,c is smooth, then from Theorem 1.8 one ﬁnds: χ(Spec Z0,c) = |W/Ad |. The result
follows.
□
Simple H0,c-modules correspoding to points of Υ−1(0) may be called nilpotent, because
they are characterized by the property that all elements of both h ⊂H0,c and h∗⊂H0,c
act by nilpotent operators in them.
Remark. For any ﬁnite Coxeter group W, results similar to Theorem 1.5 are valid if
the symmetrizer e is replaced with the antisymmetrizer e−=

Partial brackets.
A slight generalization of the construction of the Poisson brackets on
commutative algebras eH0,ce and Z0,c (see §15) can be also applied to construct bilinear
pairings:
C[h]W × H0,c −→H0,c
and
C[h∗]W × H0,c −→H0,c
,
(P, u) 7→{P, u} .
(4.17)
To deﬁne these pairings, observe that, for any (t, c) ∈C ⊕C, one has a canonical algebra
imbedding ǫt,c : C[h]W ֒→Ht,c and, moreover, the algebra ǫ0,c(C[h]W ) is central in H0,c.
Now, given an element u ∈H0,c choose an algebraic family ut ∈Ht,c such that u0,c = u.
For any z ∈C[h]W deﬁne an element {z, u} ∈H0,c to be the linear term in the expansion of
[ǫt,c(z), ut] = ǫt,c(z) · ut −ut · ǫt,c(z) at t = 0. This term is independent of the choice of the
family ut ∈Ht,c such that ut = u, since ǫ0,c(z) is a central element. For each z ∈C[h]W ,
the assignment: u 7→{z, u} gives a derivation of the algebra H0,c, which reduces, on Z0,c,
to the map given by taking the Poisson bracket (in Z0,c) with ǫ0,c(z).
A similar construction applies to the algebra C[h∗]W instead of C[h]W . The reader
should be warned that we have not constructed a ‘Poisson bracket’: Z0,c × H0,c →H0,c
since, for t ̸= 0, there is no obvious family of vector space imbeddings ǫt,c : Z0,c ֒→Ht,c.

PART 2. HARISH-CHANDRA HOMOMORPHISMS
6
The radial part construction
We begin with the following generalization of the classical construction of ‘radial part’ of
an invariant diﬀerential operator.
Given a smooth complex algebraic variety X, let D(X) denote the algebra of regular
diﬀerential operators on X. Further, given a (possibly inﬁnite dimensional) C-vector space
A, we write D(X, A) := A N D(X) for the vector space of A-valued regular diﬀerential
operators. If A is an associative C-algebra, the space D(X, A) acquires a natural associative
algebra structure, the tensor product of that on A and on D(X).
Let G be a connected reductive algebraic group over C with Lie algebra g, and T ⊂G a
maximal torus. Let g
rs ⊂g be the subset of semisimple regular elements. Let h ⊂g be the
Cartan subalgebra corresponding to T, and write h
reg = h ∩g
rs for the Zariski open subset
formed by the regular elements of h, i.e. for the complement of the root hyperplanes. Let
Ug denote the enveloping algebra of the Lie algebra g, and (Ug)ad h denote the centralizer
of h in Ug.
Then (Ug)ad h · h is a two-sided ideal in the algebra (Ug)ad h, and we set
(Ug)h = (Ug)ad h/(Ug)ad h·h. The action of the Weyl group W on h preserves the subset
h
reg, hence induces a W-action on D(h
reg) and on (Ug)h. Let D

W denote the

(Ug)h. Thus, the space V⟨0⟩has a natural (Ug)h-module structure, and the space V⟨0⟩⊗
C[h

‘The’ Harish-Chandra homomorphism.
Let Φ : D(g)g →D(h
reg)W denote the
Harish-Chandra homomorphism associated to the trivial 1-dimensional g-module C. It
is easy to see that the following deﬁnition agrees with the classical notion of the Harish-
Chandra homomorphism:
Deﬁnition 6.7 The map Φ is called ‘the’ Harish-Chandra homomorphism.
By a fundamental result of Harish-Chandra, [HC], the image of Φ consists of regular
diﬀerential operators on the whole of h. This is not true for Harish-Chandra homomor-
phisms associated to nontrivial g-modules V .
Recently, thanks to Wallach, and Levasseur-Staﬀord, the results of Harish-Chandra
have been strengthened as follows.
Let ad : g
֒→
D(g) denote the Lie algebra map
sending x ∈g to the vector ﬁeld ad x on g.
Form the left ideal D(g) · ad g ⊂D(g),
generated by the image of the map ad, and set Iad :=
 D(g) · ad g

∩D(g)g . It is clear
that Iad is a two-sided ideal in D(g)g; moreover, the Harish-Chandra homomomorphism
Φ vanishes on Iad, hence descends to a map Φ : D(g)g/Iad →D(h)W . The theorem below
summarizes the results of Wallach [Wa], and Levasseur-Staﬀord [LS1, LS2].

Deformation of the Harish-Chandra homomorphism
We would like to construct a 1-parameter deformation of the Harish-Chandra homomor-
phism. To do this, we construct a 1-parameter family of representations {Vk} k∈C of g, such
that, for any k, the space Vk⟨0⟩is 1-dimensional, and V0⟨0⟩is the trivial representation of
(Ug)h. Then we may deﬁne a deformation of Φ by setting Φκ = ΨVk .
We now produce a particular 1-parameter family of representations Vk in the case
g = gln (it turns out that such a family does not exist for Lie algebras which are not of
type A). We think of gln as the Lie algebra of linear vector ﬁelds on the vector space
Cn, with coordinates x1, ..., xn, so that Eij = xi ∂
∂xj are the matrix units forming the
standard basis of gln. Deﬁne Vk to be the C-vector space formed by all the expressions
(x1 · . . . · xn)k · P , where P is a Laurent polynomial in x1, ..., xn of total degree zero,
47


===== PDF PAGE 48 =====

with the Lie algebra gln acting by formal diﬀerentiation. Speciﬁcally, for each i, j, there
is a well-deﬁned action on the expressions above of the diﬀerential operator xi ∂
∂xj . The
assignment: Eij 7→xi ∂
∂xj
makes Vk an gln-module.
The gln-module that we need is
obtained from this one by redeﬁning the action of the center of gln. Speciﬁcally, we ﬁrst
consider Vk as an sln-module, obtained by restricting the action from gln to sln. Now,
deﬁne a new gln-action on Vk by pulling-back the sln-action via the natural Lie algebra
projection: gln ։ sln, so that the center of gln acts trivially. This is the gln-module
Vk that we wanted to produce. The representation Vk has an interpretation in terms of
twisted diﬀerential operators explained in Appendix B.
It is clear that the g-module Vk thus obtained has a 1-dimensional zero weight space
Vk⟨0⟩, spanned by the element (x1 · . . . · xn)k · 1, and V0⟨0⟩is the trivial representation of
(Ug)h.
Deﬁnition 7.1 For g = gln, or g = sln, we call Φk = ΨVk :
D(g)g →D(h
reg)W the
deformed Harish-Chandra homomorphism with parameter k ∈C.
It is clear that if k = 0, then the map Φk coincides with Φ (this follows from the fact
that any equivariant function on g
rs with values in V0 is, in eﬀect, an invariant function
taking values in the trivial 1-dimensional subrepresentation of V0).
Let Ck, denote the centralizer in

reg)W of the Calogero-Moser diﬀerential opera-

Proposition 7.2 (i) For any k ∈C, we have: Φk(∆g) = Lk.
(ii) The map Φk gives an algebra isomorphism: (Sg)g
∼

Proof. For any root α, one ﬁnds that: eαe−α

Remark. Part (i) also follows from [E, EFK]. Speciﬁcally, in [EFK] the authors consider
radial parts of diﬀerential operators on the group G, rather than on its Lie algebra, and
obtain Calogero-Moser operators with trigonometric potential k(k + 1)/ sin2(x) rather
than k(k + 1)/x2 . The rational case may be obtained by taking an appropriate limit of
the result of [EFK].

Hamiltonian interpretation:
The Ad G-action on g = sln gives rise to a Hamiltonian
G-action on T ∗g, the cotangent bundle on g, with moment map µ : T ∗g →g∗.
The
algebra homomorphism ad : Ug →D(g) may be viewed as a ”quantization” of the pull-
back morphism µ∗: C[g∗] →C[T ∗g].
Further, it is known, see e.g. [BJ], that AnnVk is the primitive ideal in Ug associated
to the ‘minimal’ coadjoint orbit Ok := {s ∈sln ≃g∗ rk
 s −(k + 1
2) · Id

= 1},
via the Geometric quantization construction.
Put Iquantum := AnnVk, and let Iclassic
denote the deﬁning ideal of the (closed) orbit Ok.
Thus, according to the ”geometric
quantization” philosophy, the algebra Ug/Iquantum should be thought of as a quantization
of C[Ok] = C[g∗]/Iclassic.
As has been discovered by Kazhdan-Kostant-Sternberg [KKS], the classical Calogero-
Moser space can be obtained as the Hamiltonian reduction: µ−1(Ok)/G, cf.
also §11
below.
The coordinate ring of µ−1(Ok)/G, an aﬃne algebraic variety, by deﬁnition
is: C[µ−1(Ok)/G] = C[T ∗g]g C[T ∗g] · µ∗Iclassic
g. Thus, the non-commutative alge-
bra D(g)g D(g) · ad(Iquantum)
g is a natural ”quantization” of the coordinate ring of the
classical Calogero-Moser space. According to Corollary 7.4, this is nothing but the image
of the deformed Harish-Chandra homomorphism.

Example: g = sl2
In the case g = sl2, we will explicitly compute the source and the target of the map Φk of
Corollary 7.4 and verify directly that both are ﬂat deformations and that this map is an
isomorphism.
We ﬁrst give an explicit description of the algebra D(g)g/
 D(g) · ad(AnnVk)
g in terms
of generators and relations. To this end, it is convenient to identify g with the Lie algebra
so3, so that the adjoint representation of g becomes the tautological representation of
so3 on C3. Thus, the algebra D(g)g gets identiﬁed with the algebra of rotation-invariant
diﬀerential operators on C3. We have the following classical result (see e.g. [Sch]).
Proposition 8.1 The algebra D(C3)so3 is generated by the squared radius r2, the second
order Laplacian ∆, and the Euler ﬁeld eu.
Proof. It is the standard result in elementary invariant theory that the commutative
algebra C[C3 ⊕C3]so3 is generated by 3 elements: r2, rp, p2, where r is the radius vector,
and p is the momentum. Now, equip D(C3)so3 with ﬁltration by the order of diﬀerential
operators. Then, we have: gr
 D(C3)so3 
= C[C3 ⊕C3]so3 , and our claim follows from its
analogue for C[C3 ⊕C3]so3 , mentioned above. □
Let e, h, f be the standard sl2-triple, g = ⟨e, h, f⟩. Proposition 8.1 shows that D(g)g
can be identiﬁed with Ug, via the map given by the assignment: −1
2r2 7→f, 1
2∆7→e.
Next, we give an explicit description of the algebra Bk in terms of generators and
relations. By the deﬁnition, Bk is generated by the operators x2 and ∂2 −k(k+1)
x2
in one
variable (∂denotes the diﬀerentiation with respect to x).
Let C = ef+fe+h2/2 ∈U(sl2) be the quadratic Casimir, and set: ck = 1
2(k−1

2).
Proposition 8.2 The assignment: f 7→−1
2x2 , e 7→1
2(∂2 −k(k+1)
x2

∼

Proof. It is straightforward to check that η extends to a surjective algebra homomor-
phism: Ug ։ Bk. To show that η is an isomorphism, write M(µ) for the Verma module
over g = sl2 with highest weight µ.
51


===== PDF PAGE 52 =====

The annihilator of the module M(k −1/2) is equal to the ideal (C −ck)·Ug ⊂Ug,
so that the algebra Ug/(C −ck)·Ug acts faithfully on this Verma module. On the other
hand, consider the Bk-module spanned by x2m−k, m = 0, 1, 2, .... It is immediate to check
that the pullback of this Bk-module via η is an sl2-module isomorphic to M(k −1/2). It
follows that the map η is injective. □
Remark. It is known (see e.g. [St]) that the algebras Ug/(C −c)·Ug, c ∈C, are pairwise
nonisomorphic. Therefore, Proposition 8.2 shows that the algebras Bk and Bl are not
isomorphic unless k = l or k = −l −1.

According to Propositions 8.1 and 8.2, we may view the deformed Harish-Chandra
homomorphism Φk : D(g)g →Bk, as a certain map: Ug −→Ug/(C −ck)·Ug. We are
going to check, by an explicit calculation of radial parts, that this map coincides with the
canonical projection: Ug ։ Ug/(C −ck)·Ug.

Proof of Theorem 7.3
In this section: g = gln, and we put Ik :=

Proof of Theorem 7.3(i) for k = 0.
By Theorem 9.1 and Theorem 6.8, we have I0 = I.
Moreover, it is known that W-invariant polynomials on h and W-invariant diﬀerential
operators with constant coeﬃcients generate D(h)W , see [Wa], which implies that B0 =
D(h)W . Thus, the Harish-Chandra homomorphism Φ gives rise to an isomorphism Φ0 :
D(g)g/I0
∼
−→B0.
Proof of part (i): general case.
For any smooth algebraic variety X with a C∗-action,
we write D(X)(l, m) ⊂D(X) for the space of diﬀerential operators of order ≤m (with
respect to the standard ﬁltration on D(X)) and of homogeneity degree l with respect to
the C∗-action. Given any subspace A ⊂D(X), we put A(l, m) := A∩D(X)(l, m). We will
apply these notations for X = g and for X = h
reg, with the natural C∗-action by dilations
in either case.
Observe that the map Φk is compatible both with the ﬁltrations and the gradings,
furthermore, for any m ≥0 and l ∈Z, the space D(g)(l, m) is ﬁnite dimensional. We
claim that, for generic k (all except countably many), one has:
 Im Φk

(l, m) = Φk
 (D(g)g/Ik)(l, m)

∀m ≥0 , l ∈Z .
(10.1)
To prove this, observe that by Theorem 6.10 we know that the map gr(Φ0) is surjective.
This implies that (10.1) holds for k = 0. Further, by semi-continuity, for generic k, one
has: dim Ik(l, m) ≥dim I0(l, m).
It follows that if an element D ∈D(g)g has degree
exactly d modulo I0, it has degree ≤d modulo Ik, for generic k. However, Φ0(D) has
degree exactly d, which implies that Φk(D) has degree ≥d, for generic k. This implies
that (10.1) must hold for generic k.
Recall that, as has been mentioned in the Remark following Theorem 7.3, it is clear
that: Bk ⊂Im Φk, for any k ∈C. It follows from equation (10.1) that, for generic k and
any m ≥0 and l ∈Z, we have: Bk(l, m) ⊂

≥dim D(g)g(l, m) −dim Ik(l, m).
Comparing this with (10.2) shows that, for generic k, all the inequalities must be equalities,
in particular: dim Ik(l, m) = dim I0(l, m), and dim Bk(l, m) = dim B0(l, m). Thus, for all

It remains to show that the image of Φk is Bk not only for generic but actually for
all k ∈C. It follows from the fundamental theorem of invariant theory (see [Wa]) that
C[h ⊕h]W is generated, as a Poisson algebra, by the two subalgebras C[h]W of invariant
polynomials on the ﬁrst and the second copies of h, respectively. Therefore, for any k,
we have C[h ⊕h]W ⊂gr(Bk), where ‘gr’ is taken with respect to the natural ﬁltration by
the order of diﬀerential operators. Also, as we mentioned, one has B0 = D(h)W , hence
gr(B0) = C[h ⊕h]W . Therefore,

On the other hand, since Bk is described by generators, the dimension dim Bk(l, m) can-
not go up at special points k (it can only go down). Therefore, (10.3) yields: dim Bk(l, m) =
dim B0(l, m), for all k. This together with the fact that Im Φk = Bk for generic k, implies
that Im Φk = Bk, for all k.
Proof of part (ii).
Write B for the C[k]-algebra whose specialization at any particular
value of the parameter k is the algebra Bk. In more detail, treat k as a variable, and
view the Calogero-Moser operator (1.16) as an element of D(h
reg)W N C[k]. Let C denote
the centraliser of this operator in D(h
reg)W
−
N C[k], and B ⊂D(h
reg)W N C[k] the algebra
generated by C and by the polynomial subalgebra C[h]W N C[k] ⊂D(h)W N C[k].
By part (i), we may think of the family of deformed Harish-Chandra homomorphisms
Φk as a single C[k]-linear homomorphism: D(g)g N C[k] →B. Let Ker be the kernel of
this homomorphism. Similarly, let I be the two-sided ideal of the algebra D(g)g N C[k]
that specialises to Ik at any particular value of the parameter k. We know that I ⊂Ker.
The claim of part (ii) amounts to showing that Ker/I is supported, as a C[k]-module, at
ﬁnitely many points.
To prove this, equip D(g)g N C[k] with the standard ﬁltration (place C[k] in degee 0).
Then, we have: gr
 D(g)g N C[k]

= C[g ⊕g]g N C[k]. The RHS here is a ﬁnitely gener-
ated algebra, since g is a reductive Lie algebra. Hence D(g)g N C[k] and
 D(g)g N C[k]

/I
are ﬁnitely generated algebras as well. Therefore, the torsion ideal in
 D(g)g N C[k]

/I is
ﬁnitely generated by the Hilbert basis theorem. Thus, this ideal is supported at ﬁnitely
many points of Spec C[k]. But, by part (i), for all k except possibly a countable sub-
set, we have: Ik = Ker(Φk). It follows that Ker/I is contained in the torsion ideal in
 D(g)g N C[k]

/I, and we are done. □

Calogero-Moser space for wreath-products.

Hamiltonian interpretation
The variety MΓ,n,c can be usefully interpreted as follows.
The symplectic form on the 2-dimensional space L and the symmetric trace form Tr :
a, b 7→Tr(ab) on EndC(Cn ⊗CΓ), make L∗N EndC(Cn ⊗CΓ), a symplectic vector space
with symplectic 2-form ωL ⊗Tr. Clearly, HomΓ
 L , EndC(Cn ⊗CΓ)

is nothing but the
subspace of Γ-invariants in L∗N EndC(Cn⊗CΓ), which is again a symplectic vector space.

The Proposition below and its proof are completely parallel to [Wi, Prop. 1.7], a result
proved by Wilson in the special case: Γ = {1}.
Proposition 11.11 For generic c ∈C(Γn), the PGΓ,n-action on the variety MΓ,n,c is free.
Furthermore, MΓ,n,c is a smooth symplectic aﬃne algebraic variety of dimension 2n.
Proof. We claim ﬁrst that if E ⊂Cn ⊗CΓ is a proper Γ-stable subspace, which is
in addition invariant under ∇i , i = 1, 2, then E = 0. If E ̸= 0, then we consider the
operator [∇1, ∇2]

E. The trace of this operator is clearly zero. We now use the following
observation that will be also exploited many times elsewhere, esp. in Appendix E. Since Γ
is ﬁnite, the character table ∥ρi(uj)∥, where ρi is the i-th irreducible character of Γ and uj
is the j-th conjugacy class in Γ, involves a ﬁnite number of algebraic numbers. Therefore,
vanishing of a linear combination of the ρi(uj)’s involving some generic parameters implies
that the coeﬃcient in front of each of the ρi(uj)’s vanishes identically. Thus, for generic
c = (k, c′), vanishing of the trace of [∇1, ∇2]

E implies that the terms in the trace involving
k, resp. c′, vanish separately.
Now, looking at c′-terms we see that E is a multiple of CΓ. Further, looking at the
k-term, we see exactly like in [Wi] that EΓ, the Γ-ﬁxed part of E, must be n-dimensional.
It follows that dim E = n · |Γ|, hence E = Cn ⊗CΓ. Schur lemma implies the freeness of
the PGΓ,n-action.
We now show that the variety MΓ,n,c is smooth. To do this, it is suﬃcient to show
that k|Γ|s ⊗eΓ + 1 ⊗c′ is a regular value of the moment map µ : ∇7→[∇1, ∇2], i.e., that
the diﬀerential dµ is surjective. But the surjectivity of the diﬀerential of a moment map
for a group action on a symplectic manifold is well-known to be equivalent to the local
freeness of the group action, established above.
60


===== PDF PAGE 61 =====

It remains to calculate the dimension of MΓ,n,c. First we calculate the dimension of
MΓ,n,c(p). Since k · |Γ| · p ⊗eΓ + 1 ⊗c′ is a regular value of the moment map, we get
dim MΓ,n,c(p) = dim µ−1(p) = dim HomΓ
 L , EndC(Cn ⊗CΓ)

−dim(pgΓ,n) .
The space HomΓ
 L , EndC(Cn ⊗CΓ)

clearly has dimension 2n2|Γ|, and, moreover, we have
shown above that the PGΓ,n(p)-action on MΓ,n,c(p) is free. Therefore, using formulas (11.7)
and (11.8), we ﬁnd
dim MΓ,n,c = dim MΓ,n,c(p) −dim PGΓ,n(p) = (2n2|Γ| −dim PGΓ,n) −dim PGΓ,n(p)
=
 2n2|Γ| −n2|Γ| + 1

−
 n2(|Γ| −1) + (n −1)2
= 2n.
□

Proof of Theorem 11.16:

σ(f) = eafe. We equip the algebra (Sym L)Γ with the ﬁltration induced by the grading.
Further, the algebra eH0,ce is ﬁltered, and we have a natural isomorphism: gr(eH0,ce) ≃

 (Sym L)Γ⊗nSn, by Lemma 11.17 applied to A = (Sym L)Γ. It follows that
the image of the map σ generates the algebra eH0,ce.

[x1, y1] = [∇x, ∇y] = (n −1) · k · |Γ|, and on which Sn acts trivially. It follows from the
