Since the image of the map σ generates the algebra eH0,ce, it follows from this formula
that the map φ∗is surjective.
We now show that φ∗is injective. Assume Ker(φ∗) ̸= 0, and let Y ⊂MΓ,n,c be the
zero variety of the ideal Ker(φ∗) ⊂C[MΓ,n,c]. Thus, Y an aﬃne subscheme in MΓ,n,c
and, moreover, dim Y < dim MΓ,n,c = dim V, since MΓ,n,c is irreducible, by Corollary
11.13. But then we have: dim Y < dim Spec Z0,c(= dim V ), hence the map φ∗: C[Y ] =
C[MΓ,n,c]/Ker(φ∗) −→Z0,c cannot be surjective, contrary to what we have proven above.
To prove part (ii) of the Theorem, we note that the ﬁltration on C[MΓ,n,c] is such that
deg(∇x) = deg(∇y) = 1. The ﬁltration on Z0,c is induced from that on H0,c. It follows
directly from the proof of part (i) that the map (φ∗)−1 : Z0,c →C[MΓ,n,c] is ﬁltration
preserving.
Next, we claim that the map φ∗is also ﬁltration preserving.
Indeed, any regular
function f on MΓ,n,c such that filt. deg(f) = d is representable as a sum of products
of traces of monomials of ∇x and ∇y, of total degree at most d. So φ∗(f) is an element
of the form φ∗(f)(E) = TrE(hf), where hf ∈H0,c(Γn) is an element of ﬁltration degree
d. Thus, it remains to check that if h ∈H0,c(Γn) and z ∈Z0,c = C[IrrepH0,c(Γn)] is the
element corresponding to the function: E →TrE(h), then filt. deg(z) ≤filt. deg(h).
But this follows from Lemma 11.18.
To complete the proof, it remains to show that the map φ respects the Poisson brackets.
First of all, the Poisson bracket {−, −}MΓ,n,c on the algebra C[MΓ,n,c] is easily seen to
have ﬁltration degree ≤−2, in the sense explained above Lemma 2.26. Transporting this
bracket to Z0,c via the isomorphism φ∗, we obtain, since the map φ∗is ﬁltration compatible,
64


===== PDF PAGE 65 =====

a Poisson bracket φ∗{−, −}MΓ,n,c on Z0,c of ﬁltration degree ≤−2 again. Hence, Lemma
2.26 implies that there exists a constant λ ∈C, such that φ∗{−, −}MΓ,n,c = λ · {−, −}Z0,c .
We now, consider the line C · c ⊂C(Γn). Hence, for any r ∈C∗we get similarly:
φ∗{−, −}MΓ,n,r·c = λ(r) · {−, −}Z0,r·c. Comparing homogeneity degrees of both sides of
this equation we conclude that the function : r 7→λ(r) must be homogeneous of degree
zero, hence a constant λ(r) = λ. To compute this constant explicitly, we let r →0. Thus,
we are reduced to the case c = 0, which is easy to calculate. Namely, on gln⊕gln = gln⊗L
introduce the symplectic 2-form Tr ⊗ωL. Then φ∗ωZ =
1
|Γ| · (Tr ⊗ωL), where ωZ is the
2-form on Spec Z0,c induced by the (nondegenerate) Poisson structure on Z0,c. Thus, the
theorem is proved.
□
Interpretation via Quiver varieties.
Let Q = Q(Γ) be a quiver obtained by
choosing an orientation on the aﬃne Dynkin graph (of ADE type) arising from the group
Γ ⊂SL2 via the McKay correspondence. Thus, the set V of vertices of Q is identiﬁed
with the set Irrep(Γ) of the isomorphism classes of simple Γ-modules, so that the trivial
representation corresponds to a special vertex 0 (in a somewhat degenerate case: Γ = 1,
the quiver Q has only one (rather than 2) vertex and one edge-loop at this vertex). Given
v ∈V, we write Vv ∈Irrep(Γ) for the corresponding Γ-module.
Let n be a positive integer, and RQn the aﬃne variety (a vector space) of representa-
tions of Q in the vector space: L

Case: Γ = {1}.
From now on we will restrict ourselves to the special case of the trivial
group Γ, so that: Γn = Sn , gΓ,n = gln, and PGΓ,n = PGLn. In this case the variety MΓ,n,c
reduces to
Mn = {(∇1, ∇2) ∈gln × gln
 [∇1, ∇2] + IdCn = rank 1 matrix}/Ad PGLn ,
(11.23)
the Calogero-Moser space, introduced by Kazhdan-Kostant-Sternberg [KKS] and Wilson
[Wi] in connection with the Calogero-Moser integrable system. We can also write: Mn =
µ−1(O)/ PGLn = µ−1(p)/ PGLn(p), where µ : gln×gln →sln , (X, Y ) 7→[X, Y ], cf. (11.6).
Consider the trivial vector bundle: Cn × µ−1(p) →µ−1(p), on µ−1(p). The ﬁber Cn
over a point (X, Y ) ∈µ−1(p) carries the tautological action of the matrices X and Y ,
such that [X, Y ] = p. The imbedding: GLn−1 ֒→GLn , A 7→A ⊕IdIm(p+Id), makes Cn =
Ker(p + Id) ⊕Im(p + Id) a GLn−1-module, and makes the vector bundle: Cn × µ−1(p) →
µ−1(p) a GLn−1-equivariant bundle, with GLn−1 acting diagonally on Cn × µ−1(p). This
GLn−1-equivariant bundle descends to a well-deﬁned algebraic vector bundle Vn on Mn =
µ−1(p)/ GLn−1, to be called the tautological vector bundle.
Write H0,c = H0,c(Sn) for the symplectic reﬂection algebra attached to the Symmetric
group Sn. Recall that the family of simple H0,c-modules forms, by Theorem 1.7, a dis-
tinguished vector bundle R on Spec Z0,c. Using the identiﬁcation φ : Spec Z0,c
∼
−→Mn,
provided by Theorem 11.16 we transport R to a vector bundle φ∗R on Mn. This vector
bundle carries a canonical Sn-action along the ﬁbers, and we proceed now to describing
some particular Sn-isotypic components of φ∗R.
For any (not necessarily irreducible) Sn-module χ, we let R(χ) = HomSn(χ, R) denote
the χ-isotypic component of the vector bundle R on Spec Z0,c. In particular, take χ to
be the reﬂection representation in Cn, the sum of the irreducible (n −1)-dimensional, and
the trivial representation 1 of Sn. Write RCn for the corresponding rank n vector bundle
on Spec Z0,c.

(using the imbedding (11.9)). It is easy to see that the vector bundle morphism Fbun inter-
twines the equivariant structures on the left and on the right via the group homomorphism
Fgroup : AutSn(CSn) ։ GLn−1 described in the previous paragraph. It follows that the
morphism Fbun descends to a vector bundle isomorphism: RSn−1

Observe next that the G-action on Ht,c arising from Theorem 5.10 is trivial on CW ⊂
H0,c, hence induces a G-action on the H0,c-module H0,ce. The latter may be viewed, by
Theorem 1.23, as a G-action on the vector bundle R on Spec Z0,c.
Recall that Berest and Wilson have deﬁned in [BW] a G-action on the Calogero-Moser
variety Mn by the formulas, cf. (5.8):
aτ,ℓ(X, Y ) = (X + τY ℓ, Y )
and
bτ,ℓ(X, Y ) = (X , Y + τXℓ)
, (X, Y ) ∈gln × gln .
They showed further that the group G acts transitively on Mn.
Repeating the proof of Proposition 7.7 in the ‘limiting’ case κ = ∞, we get
Corollary 11.34 (i) The spherical Harish-Chandra homomorphism Φ
spher
∞
: D(gln)gln →
eH0,ce intertwines the G-actions, resp. the L-actions, on D(gln)gln and on eH0,ce.
(ii) The (transitive) G-action on Mn lifts to a G-action on the vector bundle R on
Mn. A similar result holds for the Lie algebra L.

Example: sl2-case.
The algebra H0,c(A1) has 3 generators: s, x, y with deﬁning rela-
tions
s2 = 1 ,
sx = −xs ,
sy = −ys ,
[y, x] = −2s.
71


===== PDF PAGE 72 =====

In the following proposition, we collect the results about H0,c(A1). The proofs are straight-
forward and are left to the reader.
Proposition 11.35 (i) The center of H0,c(A1) is generated by the 3 elements: A =
x2 , B = y2, and C = (xy + yx)/2, which satisfy the relation:
AB = C2 −1.
Thus,
Spec Z0,c is a smooth quadric: {(a, b, c) ∈C3  ab = c2 −1}.
(ii) Let (a, b, c) ∈Spec Z0,c ⊂C3. The unique irreducible representation of H0,c(A1)
corresponding to the point (a, b, c) has a basis {v+, v−} such that:
s(v±) = ±v± , x(v+) = α · v−, x(v−) = β · v+ , y(v+) = γ · v−, y(v−) = δ · v+,
where (α, β, γ, δ) is a solution of the system of equations:
αβ = a
,
γδ = b
,
βγ = c + 1
,
αδ = c −1,
deﬁned uniquely modulo rescaling transformations: (α, β, γ, δ) 7→(t·α , t−1·β , t·γ , t−1·δ).
(iii) The vector bundle R is a direct sum of two line bundles: R = eR ⊕e−R, where
eR = O is the trivial bundle, and e−R is isomorphic to the pullback of O(1) under the
ﬁbration π : Spec Z0,c →P1, given by: π(a, b, c) = (β, δ).
(iv) The Poisson bracket on M2 is induced by a rotation-invariant symplectic form. □

There is a more geometric proof of Corollary 12.6, which does not use theorem 12.1.
It is based on the following beautiful linear algebraic lemma, see e.g., [Gu]:
Lemma 12.7 Let A, B be two n×n-matrices whose commutator, [A, B] is a rank 1 matrix.
Then A, B can be simultaneously conjugated into upper triangular matrices.
The proof of this lemma in [Gu] is rather complicated, so we would like to give a
simpler proof which we learned from A. Rudakov.
Proof. Without loss of generality we can suppose that the kernel of A is non-zero;
otherwise we can replace A by A −λ · Id, where λ is an eigenvalue of A. It is enough to
show that there is a proper subspace which is both A- and B-stable. Put C := [A, B].
If Ker A belongs Ker C then Ker A is B-invariant: ABx = BAx+Cx = 0. Thus, Ker A
is the needed subspace.
If Ker A is not in Ker C then there exists z such that Az = 0, Cz ̸= 0. This means
ABz = Cz is not zero. Hence Im C, which is a 1-dimensional space, is contained in Im A.
Now Im A is B-invariant, because BAx = ABx + Cx, and both summands are in Im A.
Thus, Im A is the needed subspace. □
We observe now that Lemma 12.7 implies Corollary 12.6. In more detail, recall the
variety Z1 =

(X, Y ) ∈g ⊕g
 rk
 [X, Y ]

≤1
	
. It follows from Lemma 12.7 that the
closure of any G-orbit in Z1 contains a point of Z (since a pair of upper triangular ma-
trices can be conjugated arbitrarily close to their diagonal parts). Hence, any g-invariant
polynomial on g ⊕g that vanishes on Z, must vanish on the whole of Z1.
□
Remarks (i) We have shown that Lemma 12.7 implies Corollary 12.6. In fact, the corol-
lary also easily implies the lemma. Indeed, it is easy to show that any two matrices the
74


===== PDF PAGE 75 =====

closure of whose GLn-orbit contains a pair of commuting matrices, generate a solvable Lie
algebra.
(ii) There is a natural analogue of the claim of Lemma 12.7 for a general semisimple
Lie algebra g saying that any pair of elements A, B ∈g such that [A, B] is a root element,
generates a solvable Lie algebra. This claim is false in such generality; it is false for the
Lie algebra g = so8, for instance.

Appendix B: Geometric construction of Vk
In this appendix we explain the geometric construction of the representation Vk over
g = sln using the notion of twisted diﬀerential operators, see e.g., [BB].
Let ω be the canonical (line) bundle on Pn−1, and let L denote its total space with
zero-section removed. Thus, L is a principal C∗-bundle on Pn−1. Write eu for the Euler
vector ﬁeld on L generating the C∗-action, and D(L)eu for the centralizer of the Euler ﬁeld
in the algebra of global algebraic diﬀerential operators on L. Note that eu is a central
element in D(L)eu. For any k ∈C, we set Dk(L) := D(L)eu/(eu −k) · D(L)eu .
Remark. The algebra Dk(L) is known as a ring of twisted diﬀerential operators on Pn−1.
In the special case when k is an integer, the algebra Dk(L) turns out to be isomorphic
to D(Pn−1, ω⊗(−k)), the algebra of global algebraic diﬀerential operators acting on the
sections of the line bundle ω⊗(−k).
♦
The torus T ⊂SLn, formed by diagonal matrices, has a unique open dense orbit in
Pn−1. Let L
reg ⊂L be the preimage of this T-orbit in L, and let Dk(L
reg) := D(L
reg)eu/(eu−
k) · D(L
reg)eu be the corresponding ring of twisted diﬀerential operators.
For each k ∈C, we now deﬁne an Ug-module Vk as follows. The tautological SLn-
action on Pn−1 lifts naturally to an action on L, that commutes with the C∗-action.
Diﬀerentiating the SLn-action gives, by restriction to L
reg, a Lie algebra morphism ̺k :
g = sln −→C∗-equivariant vector ﬁelds on L
reg. This Lie algebra morphism extends to
an associative algebra homomorphism: Ug →D(L
reg)eu , and we form the composite map:
̺k : Ug −→D(L
reg)eu ։ D(L
reg)eu/(eu −k) · D(L
reg)eu = Dk(L
reg) .
(13.1)
Let Dk(L
reg)·̺k(h) ⊂Dk(L
reg) be the left ideal generated by the image of Cartan subalgebra
h = Lie T. Thus, Dk(L
reg)/Dk(L
reg) · ̺k(h) is a left Dk(L
reg)-module, hence, an Ug-module
via (13.1). The proof of the following result is left to the reader.
Proposition 13.2 The g-module Dk(L
reg)/Dk(L
reg) · ̺k(h) is isomorphic to Vk. □

Appendix D: Small representations.
In this appendix we will consider the special case of the radial part construction of §6 and
the Dunkl-Cherednik construction in §4 in the case when V is a small ﬁnite dimensional
75


===== PDF PAGE 76 =====

representation of g.
This situation is of special interest because in the case of small
representations both constructions give the same answer.
Let g be a ﬁnite dimensional simple Lie algebra.
Let ̺ : Ug →EndV be a ﬁnite
dimensional (not necessarily irreducible) representation such that all of its weights belong
to the root lattice of g. Then, ̺ is called small if ̺(eα)2v = 0, for any root vector eα ∈g
and any v ∈V⟨0⟩. This condition is equivalent to saying that 2α is not a weight of V , for
any root α. Note that V = g, the adjoint representation of any reductive Lie algebra, is
small. We refer to the paper [Br] for more information about small representations.
For g = sln, one obtains a complete understanding of small representations as fol-
lows. Note that, for any ﬁnite-dimensional g-module V , the Weyl group W acts naturally
on V⟨0⟩.
Write Cn for the tautological representation, and (Cn)⊗n for its n-th tensor
power. We let Cat denote the (semisimple) abelian category of ﬁnite dimensional sln-
representations V such that all simple constituents of V are among the simple constituents
of (Cn)⊗n.
Proposition 14.1 (i) The representation (Cn)⊗n, and its dual, are both small.
(ii) An irreducible sln-module is small if and only if it is a submodule of either (Cn)⊗n,
or of its dual. In particular, any object of Cat is a small representation.
(iii) The assignment: V ⇝V⟨0⟩gives an equivalence between the category Cat and the
category of ﬁnite-dimensional Sn-modules. This equivalence sends (Cn)⊗n to the regular
representation of Sn.
Proof. We leave the proof to the reader, and only comment on the last statement.
Let e1, . . . , en the standard basis in Cn. It is clear that, for V = (Cn)⊗n, the element
ei1 ⊗. . . ⊗ein belongs to the zero weight subspace if and only if the indices i1, . . . , in
are pairwise distinct. Hence the space V⟨0⟩has a basis formed by the elements eσ(1) ⊗
. . . ⊗eσ(n) , σ ∈Sn. Therefore we may identify V⟨0⟩with CSn via the correspondence:
σ ←→eσ(1) ⊗. . . ⊗eσ(n) . □
Recall the Harish-Chandra homomorphism ΨV : D(g
rs)g −→D
 h
reg, EndCV⟨0⟩
W , see
(6.6). On the other hand, consider the rational Cherednik algebra H1,1, with parameters:
t = 1 and c = 1. The renormalized Dunkl homomorphism Θ1,1 : H1,1 →D(h
reg, CW), see
above formula (4.7), restricts to a homomorphism Θ1,1 : (H1,1)W →D(h

D1, D2 both have homogeneity degree −m with respect to the dilation action on h
reg.

reg of order ≤m −1, that commutes with
D and has homogeneity degree −m. The proof of the Theorem is now completed by the
following result.
Proposition 14.5 Let M be a EndCV ⟨0⟩-valued diﬀerential operator on an open subset of
h that commutes with D, has order ≤m −1, and homogeneity degree −m. Then M = 0.
The remainder of the section is devoted to the proof of Proposition 14.5. We need
an easy lemma from classical representation theory. Write E : p 7→E(p) = |p|2 for the
quadratic polynomial on h∗corresponding to ∆∈S2h, and view it as a function on h⊕h∗
constant along the ﬁrst factor.
77


===== PDF PAGE 78 =====

Lemma 14.6 Let S : (x, p) 7→S(x, p) be a rational function on h⊕h∗, which is polynomial
in p ∈h∗. Suppose that the Poisson bracket {E, S} equals to zero. Then S ∈C[h ⊕h∗].
Proof of Lemma.
We may assume without loss of generality that the function S is
homogeneous in the p-variable, say of degree N. By shifting x, we may assume further
that S is regular at x = 0. Let S = P∞
k=0 Sk be the Taylor expansion of S at the point
(0, 0) ∈h⊕h∗. Thus, Sk is a homogeneous polynomial on h⊕h∗of total degree k. Clearly,
Sk has degree N in the p-variable. Further, separating homogeneous components in the
equation {E, S} = 0 yields: {E, Sk} = 0, for any k ≥0.
The quadratic functions: E(x, p) = |p|2 , H(x, p) = ⟨x, p⟩, and F(x, p) = |x|2, (x, p) ∈
h
reg × h∗, are well-known to form an sl2-triple: {E, H, F}, with respect to the Poisson
bracket on C[h ⊕h∗]. Hence, taking the brackets with these functions gives, for each
m ≥0, an sl2-action on C[h ⊕h∗](m), the space of degree m homogeneous polynomials.
By representation theory of sl2, any vector annihilated by E (i.e. a highest weight vector)
has to have a nonnegative weight. This means that the p-degree of such a polynomial
is greater than or equal to its x-degree. In particular, the x-degree of Sk is ≤N, which
means that its total degree is ≤2N. Therefore, for k > 2N, we have Sk = 0.
□
Proof of Proposition 14.5:
Assume the contrary.
Let S : (x, p) 7→S(x, p) be the
principal symbol of M, where (x, p) ∈h⊕h∗. Then S is a polynomial in p and {E, S} = 0.
We know that S has homogeneity degree −m, in the grading where: deg(x) = 1 =
−deg(p). Furthermore, S has order ≤m−1 with respect to p. This implies that S cannot
be a polynomial in x. That contradicts the lemma, and we are done. □
15
Deformations, Poisson brackets and cohomology
Deformations and Poisson structures.
Let A be a ﬂat formal (not necessarily com-
mutative) deformation of an associative commutative algebra A, in other words, we are
given a topologically free C[[t]]-algebra A such that A/tA = A. Then, there is a canon-
ical Poisson bracket on A deﬁned as follows. First, for each eu, ev ∈A we have, since A
is commutative: [eu, ev] ∈tA, where [eu, ev] := euev −eveu. Let m(eu, ev) ≥1 be the greatest
integer (possibly = ∞) such that [eu, ev] ∈tm(eu,ev)A. Let m ≥1 be the minimum of the
integers m(eu, ev) over all pairs eu, ev as above. If m = ∞we set the Poisson bracket on A
to be zero. If m is ﬁnite then, given u, v ∈A, choose any representatives eu, ev ∈A, so
that u = eu mod (tA) and v = ev mod (tA), and put {u, v} = (t−m[eu, ev]) mod (tA). It is
known that the assignment: u, v 7→{u, v} gives rise to a well-deﬁned Poisson bracket on
A, independent of the choices involved.
Observe that the equality: m = ∞in the construction above implies [eu, ev] ∈tℓ· A,
∀eu, ev ∈A, and ∀ℓ≥1. Since T
ℓ≥1 tℓA = 0, we see that the vanishing of the Poisson
bracket on A forces the whole deformation to be commutative, i.e., we have
Lemma 15.1 If m = ∞, then A is a commutative algebra.
□
78


===== PDF PAGE 79 =====

Remark.
As has been observed by Hayashi [Ha], a slight modiﬁcation of the above
construction still applies in the case of a non-commutative algebra A, and gives a Poisson
bracket on Z(A), the center of A. We note that, as opposed to the commutative case con-
sidered above, the Hayashi construction only works if m = 1, in particular, the bracket on
Z(A) may turn out to be identically zero. This is so, for instance, if Z(A) is a specialization
of the family {Z(At)}t̸=0.
♦
From now until the end of this section we keep the following setup.
Let A be a
commutative associative C-algebra, and A a complete topological C[[t]]-algebra such that
A/t·A = A. Write K = C((t)) for the ﬁeld of formal Laurent series, and set: AK =
K b⊗C[[t]]A, a K-algebra obtained by localizing at t (here ‘ b⊗’ stands for the completed
tensor product). Let HH•(AK) denote the Hochschild cohomology of AK (relative to the
ground ﬁeld K). Further, let H•(Spec A) denote the ordinary singular cohomology (with
complex coeﬃcients) of Spec A viewed as a topological space. For any vector space E,
write E[[t]] for the space of formal power series with coeﬃcients in E.
The deformation A gives rise to a Poisson structure {−, −} on A, making Spec A
a Poisson variety. The following result is based on the celebrated Formality theorem of
Kontsevich [Kon].
Proposition 15.2 Assume that the canonical isomorphism: A/t · A
∼
−→A can be lifted
to a topological C[[t]]-module isomorphism:
φ : A
∼
−→A[[t]] .
(15.3)
If Spec A is smooth and the Poisson structure {−, −} on A is non-degenerate, i.e., makes
Spec A a symplectic manifold, then there is a graded K-algebra isomorphism: HH•(AK)
≃K ⊗C H•(Spec A).
To prove the Proposition we transport the multiplication map from A to A[[t]] via the
bijection (15.3) to obtain a C[[t]]-bilinear map ⋆: A[[t]] ⊗A[[t]] −→A[[t]] , (P, Q) 7→
P ⋆Q = φ
 φ−1(P) · φ−1(Q)

. Expanding into powers of t one can write it in the form of
a star-product
P ⋆Q = P · Q + t · µ1(P, Q) + t2 · µ2(P, Q) + . . . .
(15.4)
The algebra (A[[t]], ⋆) thus obtained is clearly isomorphic to A. The following result is
quite standard.
Lemma 15.5 Let A be a deformation of a commutative algebra A, as in Proposition 15.2.
Then there exists a ⋆-product on A[[t]] equivalent to (15.4), such that:
•
Each map µi : A × A →A in this new ⋆-product is given by a certain regular
bi-diﬀerential operator on Spec A;
•
µ1(P, Q) = {P, Q}, where {−, −} stands for the Poisson bracket on A induced by
the deformation A;
•
The C[[t]]-algebra A is isomorphic to
 A[[t]], ⋆

.

Proof of Lemma 15.5:
Put M = Spec A. Then for any k ≥0, the Hochschild co-
homology, HHk(A), are well-known to be isomorphic to, Ωk(M), the space of regular
diﬀerential k-forms on M. Let C•(A) denote the standard Hochschild cochain complex,
and C•
diﬀ(A) ⊂C•(A) the subcomplex formed by poly-diﬀerential operators. Diﬀerential
forms on M being local objects, it follows that the imbedding: C•
diﬀ(A) ֒→C•(A) is a
quasi-isomorphism. Recall that all the terms µi in the expansion (15.4) are controlled by
(second and third) Hochschild cohomology groups. Therefore, changing the star-product
by an equivalent one, one may achieve that: µi ∈C2
diﬀ(A), for all i ≥1. This proves
the claim about bi-diﬀerential operators. The claim concerning the Poisson bracket is
well-known. The Lemma is proved.
□
Proof of Proposition 15.2:
Set M := Spec A, a smooth aﬃne symplectic variety.
Hence, the Poisson structure on M is given by a regular bi-vector ﬁeld: β ∈V2 TM. Now,
ﬁx a star-product on A[[t]] as in Lemma 15.5. By (an algebraic version of) the Formality
theorem of Kontsevich [Kon], this star-product is equivalent to the one obtained by the
Kontsevich quantization procedure applied to an appropriate formal Poisson structure of
the form: bβ = β0 + t · β1 + t2 · β2 + . . . ∈(V2 TM)[[t]], such that β0 = β. Write HP •(−),
for the Poisson cohomology of a Poisson algebra, see Brylinski [Br] and references therein.
Kontsevich’s theorem on the cup-product on tangent cohomology, see [Kon, §8], yields a
graded K-algebra isomorphism:
HH•(AK) ≃HP • K[M] , bβ

.
(15.6)
Here, the RHS stands for the Poisson cohomology of the commutative K-algebra K[M] :=
Kb⊗CC[M] equipped with the K-linear Poisson structure given by bβ.
The cohomology on the RHS of (15.6) are computed by means of Kb⊗C(V• TM), the
complex formed by poly-vector ﬁelds, with diﬀerential being induced by the Schouten
bracket with the bivector bβ.
It follows, since the Poisson structure β = β0 on M
is non-degenerate and C[[t]] is a local ring, that the natural pairing with bβ induces
a C[[t]]-module isomorphism: (T ∗M)[[t]]
∼
−→(TM)[[t]], hence, a graded isomorphism:
Kb⊗C(V• T ∗M)
∼
−→Kb⊗C(V• TM). Brylinski showed [Br] that the de Rham diﬀerential
goes under the latter isomorphism to the Poisson diﬀerential. Thus, writing H•
DR(M) for
the algebraic de Rham cohomology of M one obtains a natural K-algebra isomorphism:
HP j(K[M]) ≃K ⊗C H•
DR(M). But de Rham cohomology of the smooth aﬃne variety M
is known (Grothendieck) to be isomorphic to H•(M), the ordinary singular cohomology
of M, viewed as a topological space. Combining these considerations with (15.6), we ﬁnd
HH•(AK) ≃HP • K[M] , bβ

≃K ⊗C H•
DR(M) ≃K ⊗C H•(M) .
□
In applications, Proposition 15.2 will be used in the following situation. Let A be a
ﬂat formal deformation of A, as above. Assume given an increasing ﬁltration: C[[t]] =
F0A ⊂F1A ⊂. . . , on A such that: ∪i≥0 FiA = A, and FiA · FjA ⊂Fi+jA,
∀i, j ≥0 .
The ﬁltration on A induces a ﬁltration on A = A/t·A, and we write grA, resp. grA, for
the corresponding associated graded algebra. The following result is proved by a routine
argument involving, for each i ≥0, an arbitrary choice of complementary subspace to FiA
in Fi+1A.
80


===== PDF PAGE 81 =====

Lemma 15.7 Assume that: (i) grA is a ﬁnitely generated (commutative) C-algebra, in
particular, dim FiA < ∞, for any i ≥0.
(ii) The algebra A is complete, and the isomorphism: grA/t·grA
∼
−→grA can be
lifted to a graded topological C[[t]]-algebra isomorphism: grA = (grA)[[t]] .
Then (15.3) holds.
□
Given an algebra A and an idempotent e ∈A, we say that the pair (A, e) has Morita
property if the map: A ⊗A →A given by x ⊗y →x · e · y is surjective.
A similar
deﬁnition applies for a complete topologically free C[[t]]-algebra A in which case the map:
Ab⊗C[[t]]A →A involves the completed tensor product over C[[t]].
Let A be a complete topologically free C[[t]]-algebra, and A = A/t·A.
Proposition 15.8 Let e ∈A be an idempotent in A, and e ∈A its reduction modulo t.
If (A, e) has Morita property, then so does (A, e).
Proof. This is an immediate consequence of the following general claim: Let f : V →
W be a continuous homomorphism of complete topologically free C[[t]]-modules. If the
induced map f : V/t·V →W/t·W is surjective, then f is surjective as well.
To prove the Claim, we can assume that V = V [[t]], W = W[[t]], and f = P
j≥0 tj · fj,
where fj : V →W. Suppose we want to solve the equation f(P tj · vj) = P ti · wi, where
wi are given. Suppose we’ve solved it up to order n −1, let’s solve in order n. We have
f0(vn) + known things = wn. Since f0 is surjective, we can always solve it.
□
16
Some examples
Let Γ be a ﬁnite subgroup of SL2. Consider the wreath product Γn = Sn ⋉Γn, and use
the notation as at the beginning of §9. We will consider the symplectic reﬂection algebra
H0,k,c(Γn), for generic k, c. Our ﬁrst result in this section is
Theorem 16.1 Any simple H0,k,c-module restricts to the regular representation of Γn.
This theorem and proposition 3.8 imply the following corollary:
Corollary 16.2 For wreath-products, the Calogero-Moser space Spec Z0,k,c is smooth.
□
Before proving the theorem, we prove the following lemma.
Lemma 16.3 Let E be a ﬁnite dimensional representation of H0,k,c. Then as a Γn-module,
E has the form IndΓn
SnL, where L is a unique Sn-module.

Proof of Lemma 16.3. To show that E is induced, it suﬃces to show that trE(g) = 0
if g ∈Γn is an element not conjugate to an element of Sn. We may assume that g =

Let a, b ∈C2, and consider the commutator [(a, 0, ..., 0), (b, b, . . . , b, 0, ..., 0)] in H0,k,c
(here and below, the number of copies of b in the element (b, b, . . . , b, 0, ..., 0) ∈V =
(C2)⊕n ⊂H0,k,c equals n1 =length of the cycle σ). According to the main commutation

The lemma is proved.
□
Proof of Theorem 16.1. In the previous lemma we showed that, for any γ ∈Γn, the
trace of γ in E vanishes unless γ is not conjugate to an element of Sn ⊂Γn. Thus, it
remains only to show that the trace of any nontrivial permutation σ in E vanishes.
Let us take y = (b, b, ..., b), and x = (0, ..., 0, a, 0, ..., 0), a, b ∈C2, where a stands
in the i-th place.

σ[x, y] = [σx, y] of H0,k,c. The trace of this element in E is obviously zero. Thus, writing
this trace explicitly using the main commutation relation

in a cycle), hence, it cannot be conjugated into Sn. Thus, its trace is zero by Lemma 16.3.

The last equality involves only permutations with a ﬁxed number of cycles, namely

So the terms of the sum correspond to partitions of n into m −1 parts.
In
terms of partitions, the latter sum can be expressed in a very simple way. Namely, let
the length of the cycle of σ containing i be l. Then the other cycles deﬁne a partition
µ = (µ1, µ2, ..., µm−1), µ1 ≥µ2 ≥. . . , of n −l. The last equation has the form
Xm−1

where trace of a partition is by deﬁnition the trace in E of the corresponding permutation
(to view (µ1, ..., µp + l, ..., µm−1) as a partition one may rearrange the elements involved
in the decreasing order if this happens not to be so).
Now, let λ be a partition of n into n−m−1 parts, λ = (λ1, . . . , λm−1), λ1 ≥λ2 ≥. . . .
Let p be the largest index for which λp ̸= 1. Let l = λp −1, and µ be the partition
(λ1, ..., λp −l = 1, 1, 1, . . . , 1) of n −l. Then the above equation for this µ allows one to
express Tr(λ) through traces of partitions which are bigger with respect to the standard
ordering than λ (i.e. obtainable from λ by increasing λq and decreasing λr with q < r).
This implies that Tr(λ) = 0, as desired.
□
Proposition 16.4 (i) If W is the Weyl group of type A or of type B = C, then the
variety Spec Z(H0,c) is smooth, for generic c.
(ii) If W is the Weyl group of type G2, then the variety Spec Z(H0,c) is never smooth.
Proof.
Part (i) is a special case of Corollary 16.2 with Γ = {1} for type A, and
Γ = Z/2Z, for type B. To prove (ii), by Proposition 3.8(i), it suﬃces to produce, for
Weyl group W of type G2, a simple H0,c-module of dimension less than |W|. We have
dim C = 2, hence, c = (c1, c2), where c1 ∈C and c2 ∈C correspond to long and short
roots, respectively.
Recall the structure and representation theory of W = W(G2). The group W is the
group of symmeries of the regular hexagon, so it contains rotations {rj}j=0,...,5, where r
is the rotation by π/3, and reﬂections: s0, ..., s5, where sj is the reﬂection with respect
to the line making angle πj/6 with the x-axis. The element r3 (the central symmetry) is
central in W, and W = Z/2 × S3, where Z/2 = {1, r3}, and S3 = {1, r2, r4, s0, s2, s4}.
Therefore, the group W has six irreducible representations: T+, T−, S+, S−, D+, D−,
where T, S, D are the trivial, sign, and 2-dimensional representations of S3, and ± denotes
the action of r3. In particular, h = D−, and the sign representation of W is S+. We note
that |W| = 12, and consider the following two reducible 6-dimensional representations
E+, E−of W: E+ = T+ ⊕S+ ⊕2D−, and E−= T−⊕S−⊕2D+. Clearly, E+ ⊕E−= CW.
The proof of part (ii) of Proposition 16.4 is now completed by the following result.
Proposition 16.5 Let W be of type G2 and assume c ∈C is generic enough. Then
(i) Any ﬁnite dimensional representation of H0,c is a linear combination of E+ and E−
as a W-module.
(ii) Each of the W-modules E+ and E−admits a unique extension to an H0,c-module;
in particular, E+ , E−are H0,c-modules of dimension 1
2|W|.

===== PDF PAGE 84 =====

Now we prove (ii), and introduce the structure of an H0,c-module on E+. We have
E+ = T+ ⊕S+ ⊕L ⊗D−, where L is a 2-dimensional space. An action of H0,c is deﬁned
by W-equivariant linear maps f1 : h →End(E+) and f2 : h∗→End(E+).

taking into account the W-equivariance of fj, we ﬁnd that

(aj, βj) = (bj, αj) = 0. It is not hard to show that αj, βj, aj, bj must be nonzero. Thus, by
changing scale in S+ we can make sure that bj = √−1 · aj , βj = √−1 · αj, and we have:
(aj, αj) = 0.
Next consider the main relation for [f1(v), f2(w)]. After a straightforward calculation,
this relation turns out to be equivalent to an additional requirement that a1α2 +a2α1 = 0,
and a1α2 −a2α1 = k (where k is a constant multiple of c1 + c2). There is obviously a
unique solution to these equations, up to linear transformations of L: namely, a1, a2 is the
dual basis to {2k−1α2 , −2k−1α1}.
The case of E−is treated similarly. In this case, c1 + c2 is replaced with c1 −c2. Part
(ii) is proved.

Remarks
(i) It is easy to check that in E± the right hand side of the main relation
depends only on c1 ± c2 since the sums which are multiplied by c1, c2 are the same in E+
and diﬀer by sign in E−. Therefore, if f+, f−∈CW are the idempotents corresponding
to E+, E−then one has: f± · H0,c1,c2(G2) · f± = C[Z/2] ⋉
 f± · H0,c1±c2(A2) · f±

. Thus in
eﬀect we are taking a 6-dimensional representation of H0,l(A2) (regular as S3-module, as
it must be), which is compatible with the action of the central element of W. The claim
we proved is that such a module exists and is unique.
(ii) Similar result holds more generally for W = Wn being the Coxeter group of the
symmetries of a regular n-gon, whenever n ≥5 (with the G2-case corresponding to n = 6).
Speciﬁcally, the algebra H0,c(Wn) always has a simple module whose restriction to CWn is
isomorphic to a direct sum of the trivial representation, the sign representation, and of two
copies of the 2-dimensional reﬂection-representation. Thus, H0,c(Wn) has a 6-dimensional
module, hence the variety Spec Z0,c(Wn) is not smooth, if n ≥5.
(iii) Note that our result on singularity of Spec Z0,c(G2) agrees perfectly with a similar
result for the G2-type Hilbert scheme, obtained in [Ka2].
♦

Open questions
Throughout this section (V, ω) is a ﬁnite dimensional symplectic vector space over C, and
Γ ⊂Sp(V ) is a ﬁnite group generated by symplectic reﬂections and, moreover, the triple
(V, ω, Γ) is assumed to be indecomposable. We will identify V ∗with V without further
notice. Recall that Z0,c := Z(H0,c). We have, cf. §16:
Question 17.1
For which groups Γ is the algebraic variety Spec Z0,c smooth, at least for
generic values of c ∈C ?
Recall that if Spec Z0,c is smooth then the Poisson structure on Z0,c is induced by a
symplectic form, by Lemma 3.5(ii).
Question 17.2
Assume that c ∈C is such that Spec Z0,c is smooth. Is there a natural
hyper-K¨ahler structure on Spec Z0,c compatible with the holomorphic symplectic 2-form ?
Question 17.3
For which ﬁnite groups Γ ⊂Sp(V ) the dimensions of the Poisson coho-
mology groups of the Poisson algebra C[V ]Γ are given by: dim HP 2i(C[V ]Γ) = n(i), and
dim HP
odd(C[V ]Γ) = 0 ?
An argument analogous to our proof of Theorem 2.16 yields the following
Proposition 17.4
If the answer to question 17.3 is aﬃrmative for Γ, then the family
{Z0,c}c∈C gives a universal deformation of (SV )Γ in the class of Poisson algebras.
□
Problem 17.5
Study representation theory of the algebra Ht,c. Speciﬁcally, describe the
set of those (t, c) ∈C ⊕C such that:
(i) The algebra Ht,c is not simple, and/or has modules whose Gelfand-Kirillov dimension
is less than 1
2 dim V .
(ii) The algebra Ht,c has ﬁnite-dimensional modules.

===== PDF PAGE 86 =====

For rational Cherednik algebras, problem 17.5(ii) has been studied by Cherednik (see e.g.
[ChM] for the sl2-case).
Let Q denote the skew ﬁeld of fractions of the Weyl algebra A1(V ).
The group Γ
acts naturally on Q by algebra automorphisms. We have the following analogue of the
Gelfand-Kirillov conjecture
Conjecture 17.6
For any (t, c) ∈C∗⊕C, there is an isomorphism between Q#Γ and
the Goldie ring of fractions of the algebra Ht,c, which is identical on the subalgebra CΓ.
This would imply the following result
Proposition 17.6*
If Conjecture 17.6 holds then, for any (t, c) ∈C∗⊕C, the skew ﬁeld
of fractions of the algebra eHt,ce is isomorphic to QΓ.
A ‘quasi-classical’ analogue of Proposition 17.6* is
Conjecture 17.7
For any c ∈C, the variety Spec Z0,c is birationally isomorphic to V/Γ
as a Poisson variety.
We remark that the existence of a (faithful) Dunkl-type representation, see Proposition
4.11, for the rational Cherednik algebra H0,c associated with any complex reﬂection group
Γ yields (without assuming Conjecture 17.6):
Proposition 17.7*
Proposition 17.6* and Conjecture 17.7 hold true in the case where
Γ is a complex reﬂection group in h, and V = h ⊕h∗.

Appendix G: Results of Kostant and shift operators
The notion of shift operator is quite important in the theory of Calogero-Moser systems.
The goal of this Appendix is to place this notion in the framework of Lie theory.
Fix g, a semisimple Lie algebra with a triangular decomposition g = n−⊕h ⊕n. Let
prh : Sg ։ Sg/Sg·(n + n−) = Sh be the corresponding projection. Set b = h + n, the
ﬁxed Borel subalgebra in g. Write ρ for the half-sum of positive roots, and ˇδ ∈Sh for the
product of positive coroots. Let Lµ denote the irreducible g-module with highest weight µ.
Below g = sln. Let H1,k be the rational Cherednik algebra associated with the root
system of sln. We view the family {H1,k}k∈C as a single ﬂat C[k]-algebra. Also, we view
ˇδ as an element ˇδ = δy ∈Sh ⊂H1,k.

Remark. In fact, Opdam considers the trigonometric Calogero-Moser system, while we
are considering its rational degeneration. Let us point out that the results of Opdam are
valid in the rational limit, while our construction below easily generalizes to the trigono-
metric case by replacing the Lie algebra with the corresponding group.

Before entering the proof of the Theorem, we are going to put it into a more general
framework of Kostant’s results. It is worth noticing that Kostant’s motivation had nothing
to do with the theory of Calogero-Moser systems and shift operators.
Kostant results.
We keep the notation as at the beginning of this section. In particular,
let g = n−⊕h ⊕n be an arbitrary semisimple Lie algebra with a ﬁxed triangular decom-
position, and b = h + n. Set m = number of positive roots, so that dim g = rk g + 2m.
Write Λ•g and Λ•g∗for the exterior algebras over g and g∗, respectively.
Let Ab denote the set parametrising all the abelian ideals a ⊂b having dimension
rk g.
For each a ∈Ab, the element Λrk ga ∈Λrk gg is clearly an ad b-weight vector,
hence a highest weight vector of a simple g-submodule in Λrk gg.
The corresponding
highest weight, µa, equals the sum of all the positive roots whose root vectors form a
basis of a. Further, the Hodge ‘star-operator’ relative to an invariant form on g gives
a g-equivariant isomorphism ⋆: Λrk gg
∼
−→Λdim g−rk gg = Λ2mg. We let La ⊂Λ2mg
denote the simple g-submodule corresponding to the g-submodule in Λrk gg generated by
Λrk ga. Thus, La ≃Lµa; moreover, all the modules La , a ∈Ab, are known to be pairwise
nonisomorphic.
Write Λ
evg∗⊂Λ•g∗for the even part, which is a commutative algebra. Let d : g∗−→
Λ2g∗denote the map dual to the commutator map: g ∧g

The map d can

Theorem 18.2 ([Ko2],[Ko3]) (i) The maximal i such that A2ig ̸= 0 equals m.
(ii) A2mg = L
a∈Ab La.
(iii) A simple g-module occurs in Lρ ⊗Lρ with non-zero multiplicity if and only if it
occurs in Λ•g. Thus, La occurs in Lρ ⊗Lρ with non-zero multiplicity, for any a ∈Ab.
□
Example:
sln-case.
In this case m = n(n −1)/2. The root vectors in any Young
diagram occupying the upper right hand corner of the Borel subalgebra of the upper
triangular matrices spans an ideal in the Borel subalgebra, where the Young diagram

progresses downward and to the left of E1n. If there are exactly n−1 boxes then the ideal
is abelian. Given such a Young diagram, the sum of the roots corresponding to the boxes
of the diagram form the corresponding dominant weight µa.

Proof (after Kostant).
Recall the standard Koszul diﬀerential d on Λ•g∗. Note that
the algebra A(g) is generated by exact 2-forms, relative to d. Hence, any element of A(g)
is exact. It follows that, for any ad g-invariant u ∈Sg∗, the element s(u) is ad g-invariant
and exact. But it is well-known that any non-zero ad g-invariant in Λ•g∗represents a
non-zero cohomology class in H∗(g), so that any exact ad g-invariant must be equal to
zero. Thus, s must vanish on the ideal I ⊂Sg generated by the ad g-invariants of positive
degree. Part (i) follows from this, by duality.
Let ω ∈Λ2mg be deﬁned as the wedge product of all the root vectors, with respect to
the triangular decomposition. It is a zero weight vector which transforms according to the
sign representation of the Weyl group since any simple reﬂection clearly takes ω to −ω.
Identify g∗with g and h∗with h. It follows that the element prh

Thus, the operator Ψ(Sa) satisﬁes all the properties of the shift operator. Therefore,
it must coincide with the shift operator, by the uniqueness results, due to Opdam.
